Density of subalgebras of Lipschitz functions in metric Sobolev spaces and applications to Wasserstein Sobolev spaces
Massimo Fornasier, Giuseppe Savaré, Giacomo Enrico Sodini

Abstract
. We prove a general criterion for the density in energy of suitable subalgebras of Lipschitz functions in the metric Sobolev space H 1 , p ​ ( X , 𝖽 , 𝔪 ) H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m}) associated with a positive and finite Borel measure 𝔪 \mathfrak{m} in a separable and complete metric space ( X , 𝖽 ) (X,{\sf d}) . We then provide a relevant application to the case of the algebra of cylinder functions in the Wasserstein Sobolev space H 1 , 2 ​ ( 𝒫 2 ​ ( 𝕄 ) , W 2 , 𝔪 ) H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m}) arising from a positive and finite Borel measure 𝔪 \mathfrak{m} on the Kantorovich-Rubinstein-Wasserstein space ( 𝒫 2 ​ ( 𝕄 ) , W 2 ) (\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2}) of probability measures in a finite dimensional Euclidean space, a complete Riemannian manifold, or a separable Hilbert space 𝕄 \mathbb{M} . We will show that such a Sobolev space is always Hilbertian, independently of the choice of the reference measure 𝔪 \mathfrak{m} so that the resulting Cheeger energy is a Dirichlet form. We will eventually provide an explicit characterization for the corresponding notion of 𝔪 \mathfrak{m} -Wasserstein gradient, showing useful calculus rules and its consistency with the tangent bundle and the Γ \Gamma -calculus inherited from the Dirichlet form.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 46E36, 31C25 ; Secondary: 49Q20, 28A33, 35F21, 58J65

1.
Introduction

The theory of Sobolev spaces associated to a metric measure space

(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
has been much developed in recent years. One of the most important
approaches (we refer to
the monographs
[
12
,
28
]
and to the lecture notes
[
26
,
44
]
)
is based on the notion of upper gradient

[
27
,
29
]
of a map
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
:
it is a Borel map
g:X\to[0,+\infty]
satisfying

(1.1)

|f(\gamma(b))-f(\gamma(a))|\leq\int_{\gamma}g

along every
{\sf d}
-Lipschitz (or even rectifiable) curve

\gamma:[a,b]\to X
.
The Dirichlet space
D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
,
p\in(1,+\infty)
can then be defined as the
class of measurable functions
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
that possess a
p
-integrable upper
gradient, thus resulting in a finite
Newtonian energy

(1.2)

\mathsf{N\kern-2.5ptE}_{p}(f):=\inf\Big\{\int_{X}g^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}:\text{$g$ is an upper gradient
of $f$}\Big\}.

A crucial and nontrivial fact is that
\mathsf{N\kern-2.5ptE}_{p}
admits a local
representation

(1.3)

\mathsf{N\kern-2.5ptE}_{p}(f)=\int_{X}|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{N}^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}

in terms of the
minimal
p
-weak upper gradient

|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{N}^{p}
of
f
, which can be characterized in terms of the upper gradient
property (
1.1
) along
\mathrm{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p}
-a.e. curve and enjoys
many nice metric-nonsmooth calculus rules.
When
f
is Lipschitz, then the pointwise and asymptotic Lipschitz constants

(1.4)

|{\mathrm{D}}f|(x):=\limsup_{y\to x}\frac{|f(y)-f(x)|}{{\sf d}(x,y)},\quad\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f(x):=\limsup_{y,z\to x,\ y\neq z}\frac{|f(y)-f(z)|}{{\sf d}(y,z)}

are upper gradients, so that

(1.5)

\text{if $f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)$ then}\quad|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{N}\leq|{\mathrm{D}}f|\leq\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in $X$}.

Functions in
L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
which admit a good representative
(in the usual Lebesgue class defined up to
\mathfrak{m}
-negligible sets) in

D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
give raise to the Newtonian spaces
\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})

[
45
]

[
12
, Def. 1.19]
, which
is a Banach space with the norm
\|f\|_{\hat{N}^{1,p}}:=\big(\|f\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\mathsf{N\kern-2.5ptE}_{p}(f)\big)^{1/p}
.
\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
can also be
identified with the domain of the
L^{p}
-relaxation of
\mathsf{N\kern-2.5ptE}_{p}

[
15
]
.

Density of Lipschitz
functions: the case of doubling spaces supporting a Poincaré inequality

It is a natural question if

\mathsf{N\kern-2.5ptE}_{p}
can be recovered starting from the distinguished class of
upper gradients given by the pointwise or asymptotic
Lipschitz constants (
1.4
)
of Lipschitz functions.
When
(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
satisfies a doubling condition and supports a
p
-Poincaré inequality,
then Lipschitz functions are dense in
\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})

[
45
, Theorem 4.1]

and for Lipschitz functions the minimal
p
-weak upper gradient
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{N}

coincides with the pointwise Lipschitz constant
|{\mathrm{D}}f|

[
15
, Theorem 6.1]
. In particular
for every
f\in\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
there exists a sequence

f_{n}\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
such that

(1.6)

f_{n}\to f,\quad|{\mathrm{D}}f_{n}|\to|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{N}\quad\text{strongly in }L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m}).

It is worth noticing that in this case
\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
is a
reflexive space
[
15
, Theorem 4.48]
.

Density in energy of subalgebras of Lipschitz
functions

The strong approximation property (
1.6
)
holds in fact for arbitrary complete and separable
metric spaces, a result obtained in
[
7
]
(when
p=2
)
and
[
5
]
, where also the approximation by
the asymptotic Lipschitz constant is considered.

The first aim of the present paper is to discuss the extension of this
result when the sequence
f_{n}
in (
1.6
) is chosen in a
suitable unital subalgebra
\mathscr{A}\subset\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)

separating the points of
X
, i.e.

(1.7)

1\in\mathscr{A},\quad\text{for every $x_{0},x_{1}\in X$ there exists $f\in\mathscr{A}$:\quad$f(x_{0})\neq f(x_{1})$}.

The use of a subalgebra is not a formal exercise with no
implications. In fact, in relevant examples such as
Wasserstein Sobolev spaces, which we shall introduce and discuss below, and the related subalgebra of cylinder functions, it is possible to recover the Cheeger energy as suitable relaxation of an
explicitly computable Dirichlet form
. Namely, from the so-called pre-Cheeger energy

(1.8)

\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f):=\int_{X}(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f)^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m},\quad f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X),

we recover the Cheeger energy as its relaxation starting from
\mathscr{A}
:

(1.9)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f)=\inf\left\{\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f_{n}):f_{n}\in\mathscr{A},\,f_{n}\to f\text{ in }L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})\right\}.

Thanks to the algebraic properties of
\mathscr{A}
and
(
1.7
)
it is possible to prove
[
44
, Sec. 3]
that
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}
admits a local
representation
of the form

(1.10)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f)=\int_{X}|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}^{p}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)\quad\text{whenever }\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f)<+\infty,

in terms of a minimal
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient

|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}
, enjoying the same calculus rules as
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{N}
, see Theorem
2.3
below.
We denote by
H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A})
the class of functions in
L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
with finite
(p,\mathscr{A})
-Cheeger energy.

It is easy to check that
H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A})\subset\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
with

|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{N}\leq|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
It turns out that the strong approximation property

(1.11)

\text{for every }f\in\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})\ \text{there exist }f_{n}\in\mathscr{A}:\ \ f_{n}\to f,\ \mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f_{n}\to|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{N}\text{ in }L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})

is equivalent to the identification

(1.12)

H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A})=\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m}),\quad|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{N}=|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\quad\text{for every }f\in\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m}).

The density results of
[
7
,
5
]

show that (
1.12
) always hold if
\mathscr{A}=\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
.
When
\mathscr{A}
is a proper subalgebra of
\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
,
a first sufficient condition for the validity of (
1.12
), in the more general framework of
extended topological metric measure spaces,
is provided by the
compatibility condition between
{\sf d}
and
\mathscr{A}

[
44
, Theorems 3.2.7, 5.3.1]

(1.13)

{\sf d}(x,y)=\sup\Big\{f(x)-f(y):f\in\mathscr{A},\ \mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(f,X)\leq 1\Big\}.

We are able to improve (
1.13
) and to show (Theorem

2.13
) that a necessary and
sufficient condition for (
1.12
) is that
for every
y\in X
(or in a dense subset of
X
) the distance function

{\sf d}_{y}:x\mapsto{\sf d}(x,y)

satisfies

(1.14)

|{\mathrm{D}}{\sf d}_{y}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(x)\leq 1\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $x\in X$}.

As mentioned above, the density of distinguished subalgebras of
Lipschitz functions can provide valuable information on the structure
of the metric Sobolev space
\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
, in particular
when the asymptotic Lipschitz constant
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f
exhibits a more
regular behaviour when restricted to
\mathscr{A}
.
A relevant example arises when an algebra
\mathscr{A}
exists for which
the pre-Cheeger energy (
1.8
) is induced by a bilinear
form and one wants to study its closure
as it is typical in the theory of Dirichlet forms. In this case our
result shows that this construction is intrinsically linked to the
metric structure, so that it is independent of the particular choice
of the algebra
\mathscr{A}
satisfying (
1.14
) and it is invariant with respect to measure-preserving
isometries.
As a byproduct, we will recover in a simple way previous Hilbertianity
results of

[
21
,
31
,
44
]
.

The Wasserstein Sobolev space

An important application, which has been one of the inspiring motivations of
our investigation,
concerns functional analysis over spaces of probability measures. In fact, smooth functions do appear recently as solutions of new types of partial differential equations over spaces of probability measures defined by diverse forms of differentiation, namely nonlinear transport equations
[
2
,
3
]
for describing population evolutionary games, Kolmogorov equations
[
35
,
34
]
in nonlinear filtering, and Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equations
[
11
,
23
,
39
,
14
]
as appearing, e.g., in the theory of mean-field games and mean-field optimal control.
In some of these instances, the solutions are considered in classical sense, because of the lack of weak formulations and variational descriptions. Moreover, while the expression of these equations is in most cases of foundational interest, their relevance in terms of providing insights about solutions and their explicit computation remained so far rather unclear.
Hence, a proper definition of function spaces of regular functions, rules of calculus, and density properties are fundamental for developing a more systematic framework for the analysis of such novel forms of infinite dimensional PDEs and explaining their practical use and impact.

Moreover, thanks to significant advances in computational optimal transport that made its numerical realization feasible also for problems of relatively high dimension, in the past decade there has been an increasing and more accepted adoption of probability measures to model data points in image and shape processing and other machine learning applications. While the first applications were about discriminating data encoded as distributions available in the form of bags-of-features or descriptors, more recent developments explored geometric interpolation of data provided by optimal transport, for instance in the form of Wasserstein barycenters. In the meanwhile the literature on the subject has grown significantly to be really able to offer a complete account and we may more simply refer to the recent survey
[
38
]
for insights and references.

Building upon these advances, approximating or interpolating efficiently functions over data points modeled as (probability) measures can also provide a novel framework for machine learning tasks, such as classification and regression. Also for such developments a proper foundation of functional analysis is necessary.

These are relevant motivations for us to focus on the study of Sobolev spaces generated by a finite measure
\mathfrak{m}
on the
Wasserstein space
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})
of Borel probability measures in
a complete Riemannian manifold
(\mathbb{M},{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}})
with finite quadratic
moment

(1.15)

\int_{\mathbb{M}}{\sf d}^{2}_{\mathbb{M}}(x,x_{o})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)<+\infty\quad\text{for some, and thus any, $x_{o}\in\mathbb{M}$},

endowed with the

L^{2}
-Kantorovich-Rubinstein-Wasserstein distance
W_{2}

(1.16)

W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}}^{2}(\mu,\nu):=\min\left\{\int_{\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}}{\sf d}^{2}_{\mathbb{M}}(x,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}(x,y)\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)\right\};

here
\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
is the set of couplings between
\mu
and

\nu
, i.e. probability measures
\mu
in
\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}
whose
marginals are
\mu
and
\nu
.

The space of probability measures
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}})

may be considered a model class for the above mentioned applications and it is an example of complete and separable metric
space, which exhibits a non-smooth, infinite dimensional
pseudo-Riemannian character

[
37
,
4
,
49
]
. In particular, it is not isometric to a
finite dimensional Riemannian manifold or a
\mathrm{Cat}(\kappa)

space
[
20
]
; when
\mathbb{M}
has nonnegative
sectional curvature as in the case of the Euclidean space

\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}})
has nonnegative curvature
in the sense of Aleksandrov
[
49
]
; for a general Riemannian
manifold
\mathbb{M}
,

(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}})
is not an Aleksandrov space and lacks of
any lower or upper curvature bound.

When
\mathbb{M}
is compact,
Sobolev spaces on
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2})
have been constructed in
[
17
]
starting
from measures
\mathfrak{m}
which have full support and satisfy an integration-by-parts formula
(see Section
5.2
below)
on the unital algebra of cylinder maps
\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})\right)
, generated by linear
functionals of the form

(1.17)

\mathsf{L}_{\phi}:\mu\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{M}}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu,\quad\phi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{M}).

It turns out that the restriction of the pre-Cheeger energy
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
(
1.8
)
to
\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})\right)
is induced by a
bilinear form
so that one can study the Dirichlet form arising by its closure.

Our main result is that for every separable and complete Riemannian manifold
\mathbb{M}
and for every

positive and finite Borel measure
\mathfrak{m}
on

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})
(so, full support and integration-by-parts properties are not required)
the algebra
\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})\right)
satisfies
property (
1.14
) and therefore it is dense in the metric
Sobolev space
H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})
, which is
therefore a Hilbert space.
Due to the non-smooth character of
W_{2}
such a Hilbertianity
property was far from obvious even in the flat case
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We will also show that this result holds when
\mathbb{M}
is an
infinite-dimensional, separable, Hilbert space.

Our metric analysis is also supplemented with a detailed discussion of
the structure of the Cheeger energy and of the minimal relaxed gradient,
in the case when
\mathbb{M}
is the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Introducing the measure
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}=\int\big(\delta_{\mu}\otimes\mu\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)
in
\mathcal{P}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we
will show that there is a linear continuous Wasserstein-gradient
operator
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}:H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
representing the bilinear form
associated to the Cheeger energy as

(1.18)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F,G)=\int{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)\cdot{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}G(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x),

and satisfying useful calculus rules which are typical of

\Gamma
-calculus for Dirichlet form.
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
also allows for an explicit
characterization of the
tangent bundle
L^{2}\big({\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
in the sense of Gigli

[
25
,
26
]
.

We are also able to study the relaxation effect occurring in the
construction of the Cheeger energy starting from
(
1.8
).
We claim that our results
are sufficiently strong and provide useful tools to pave the way for
further studies on the structure and the promising applications of
Wasserstein Sobolev spaces.
In particular, the techniques developed in the present paper can also be
applied
to study the general class of Wasserstein Sobolev spaces

H^{1,q}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{M}),W_{p},\mathfrak{m})
, with
p,q\in(1,+\infty)
,
a topic that has been addressed in
[
46
]
.

Moreover, as a direct consequence of our results, the recovery of the Cheeger energy in terms of relaxation of the explicitly computable
pre-Cheeger energy
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
(
1.8
)
on
\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})\right)
does allow the equally explicit formulation of Euler-Lagrange equations of properly formulated variational problems defined on
H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})
, which can be solved numerically over finite dimensional suitably graduated approximations of
\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})\right)
, as a sort of (nonlinear) Galerkin approximation. Hence, as a concluding remark, perhaps surprisingly, the use of the subalgebra of cylindric functions
\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})\right)
instead of
\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})\big)
as a fundamental nucleus to define Wasserstein Sobolev spaces allows to bring the theory from its foundational level to rather concrete applicability. In particular, we have in mind the above mentioned applications to the solutions of PDEs over
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})
and machine learning.

Plan of the paper

After a quick review of the construction of the Cheeger energy
starting from a subalgebra
\mathscr{A}
,
Section
2

is devoted to prove our main density result under condition
(
1.14
)
(Section
2.2
). The last part
2.4

extends the applicability of the results to a larger class of distances:
one of its quite useful applications will concern the
extension of the results for the Wasserstein Sobolev
spaces modeled on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the general case of

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})
for a complete Riemannian manifold
\mathbb{M}
, which will be
carried out in Sections
6.1
and
6.2
.

We will recap a few properties of the Wasserstein distance in
Section

3
.

Section
4
contains
a collection of some properties of cylinder functions, of their
asymptotic Lipschitz constants (Section
4.1
), and
our main density and
Hilbertianity result for the Wasserstein Sobolev space

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})
(Theorem
4.10
).

Calculus rules for the
\mathfrak{m}
-differential are presented in

Section
5
; the structure of the tangent
bundle, the properties of the residual differentials, and the study of the relaxation
effect are discussed in Section
5.1
, together with a
few examples in Section
5.2
.

The last
Section
6
shows how to
extend the result of Section
4
from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to an
arbitrary complete Riemannian manifold
\mathbb{M}
and to a separable Hilbert
space
\mathbb{H}
.

2.
Metric Sobolev spaces and density of unital algebras

In this section we will briefly recap the construction of metric
Sobolev spaces adapting the relaxation viewpoint of the Cheeger
energy to the presence of a distinguished algebra of Lipschitz
functions
[
7
,
5
,
44
]
.

2.1.
Sobolev functions and minimal relaxed gradients

Let
(X,\mathsf{d})
be a complete and separable metric space.
We will denote by
\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X,{\sf d})

the space of bounded and Lipschitz real
functions
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
. The asymptotic Lipschitz constant of

f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X,{\sf d})
is defined as

(2.1)

\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{\sf d}f(x):=\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(f,{\mathrm{B}}(x,r),{\sf d})=\limsup_{y,z\to x,\ y\neq z}\frac{|f(y)-f(z)|}{{\sf d}(y,z)},

where
{\mathrm{B}}(x,r)
denotes the open ball centered at
x
with
radius
r
and,
for
A\subset X
, the quantity
\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(f,A,{\sf d})
is defined as

\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(f,A,{\sf d}):=\sup_{x,y\in A,\,x\neq y}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{{\sf d}(x,y)}.

We will simply write
\mathop{\rm Lip}_{b}\nolimits(X),\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f,\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(f,A)
, omitting to
explicitly mention
{\sf d}
, when the
choice of the metric
{\sf d}
is clear from the context.

We will also deal with
a unital algebra

\mathscr{A}\subset\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)

separating the points of
X
, i.e.

(2.2)

1\in\mathscr{A},\quad\text{for every $x_{0},x_{1}\in X$ there exists $f\in\mathscr{A}$:\quad$f(x_{0})\neq f(x_{1})$}.

The initial Hausdorff topology
\tau_{\mathscr{A}}
induced on
X
by
\mathscr{A}
is clearly
coarser than the metric topology of
X
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}
be a finite and positive Borel measure on
X

(being
X
a Polish space,
\mathfrak{m}
is also a Radon measure).
We will denote by
{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})

the set of
\mathfrak{m}
-measurable real
functions defined in
X
;
L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
is the usual quotient of

{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})

obtained by identifying two functions which coincide
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. in

X
. In a similar way,

{\mathcal{L}}^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
and
L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
are the usual Lebesgue spaces of

p
-summable
\mathfrak{m}
-measurable (equivalence classes of) real functions,

p\in[1,+\infty]
.
It is worth noticing that by
[
44
, Lemma 2.1.27]
we have that

(2.3)

\begin{split}&\quad\quad\quad\text{ for every $p\in[1,\infty)$ and every $f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})$ taking values in an interval $I\subset\mathbb{R}$}\\
&\text{there exists a sequence $(f_{n})_{n}\subset\mathscr{A}$ with values in $I$ converging to $f$ in $L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})$}.\end{split}

We will endow
L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
with the topology of the convergence
in measure, which is induced by the metric

(2.4)

\mathrm{d}_{L^{0}}(f_{1},f_{2}):=\int_{X}\vartheta(|f_{1}-f_{2}|)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m},\quad f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m}),

where
\vartheta:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
is any increasing,
concave, bounded function with

\vartheta(0)=\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\vartheta(r)=0
.
In the following we fix an exponent
p\in(1,+\infty)
.

Definition 2.1

(
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient)
.

We say that
G\in L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
is a
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient of
a
\mathfrak{m}
-measurable function
f\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
if
there exists a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathscr{A}
such that:

(1)

f_{n}\to f
in
\mathfrak{m}
-measure and

\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f_{n}\to\tilde{G}
weakly in
L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
;

(2)

\tilde{G}\leq G

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. in
X
.

The minimal
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient of
f
(denoted by

|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}
) is the element of minimal
L^{p}
-norm among all
the
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient of
f
.
We will just write
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}
if
\mathscr{A}=\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
.

Remark 2.2
.

Notice that the minimal relaxed gradient
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}
depends also on
p\in[1,+\infty)
, see e.g.
[
6
,
12
,
28
]
. Since it will be always clear from the context which value of
p
we are considering (a general one or, in the second part of the paper,
p=2
), we omit to write explicitly this dependence.

We collect in the following Theorem
the main properties of
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}
we will extensively use.

Theorem 2.3
.

(1)

The set

S:=\Big\{(f,G)\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})\times L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m}):\text{$G$ is a $(p,\mathscr{A})$-relaxed gradient of $f$}\Big\}

is convex and it is closed with respect to
to the product topology of the convergence in
\mathfrak{m}
-measure and
the weak convergence in
L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
.
In particular, the restriction
S_{q}:=S\cap L^{q}(X,\mathfrak{m})\times L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
is weakly closed in

L^{q}(X,\mathfrak{m})\times L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
for every
q\in(1,+\infty)
.

(2)

(Strong approximation) If

f\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
has a
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient then

|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}
is well defined. If
f
takes values in a closed
(possibly unbounded)
interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
then
there exists a sequence
f_{n}\in\mathscr{A}
with values in
I

such that

(2.5)

f_{n}\to f\text{ $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in $X$},\quad\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f_{n}\to|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\text{ strongly in }L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m}).

If moreover
f\in L^{q}(X,\mathfrak{m})
for some
q\in[1,+\infty)
then
we can also find a sequence as in (
2.5
) converging strongly
to
f
in
L^{q}(X,\mathfrak{m})
.

(3)

(Pointwise minimality)
If
G
is a
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient of

f\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
then
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq G

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. in
X
.

(4)

(Leibniz rule)
If
f,g\in L^{\infty}(X,\mathfrak{m})
have
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient, then

h:=fg
has
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient and

(2.6)

|{\mathrm{D}}(fg)|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq|f|\,|{\mathrm{D}}g|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}+|g|\,|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in }X.

(5)

(Sub-linearity)
If
f,g\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
have
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, then

(2.7)

|{\mathrm{D}}(\alpha f+\beta g)|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq|\alpha|\,|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}+|\beta|\,|{\mathrm{D}}g|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in }X.

(6)

(Locality)
If
f\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
has a
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient, then
for any
\mathscr{L}^{1}
-negligible Borel subset
N\subset\mathbb{R}
we have

(2.8)

|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}=0\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ on $f^{-1}(N)$}.

(7)

(Chain rule)
If
f\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
has a
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient and
\phi\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(\mathbb{R})

then
\phi\circ f

has
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient and

(2.9)

|{\mathrm{D}}(\phi\circ f)|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq|\phi^{\prime}(f)|\,|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in }X,

and equality holds in (
2.9
) if
\phi
is monotone or

{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
.

(8)

(Truncations) If
f_{j}\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
has
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient,
j=1,\cdots,J
,
then
also the functions
f_{+}:=\max(f_{1},\cdots,f_{J})
and

f_{-}:=\min(f_{1},\cdots,f_{J})
have
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient
and

(2.10)

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{D}}f_{+}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}=|{\mathrm{D}}f_{j}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ on }\{x\in X:f_{+}=f_{j}\},

(2.11)

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{D}}f_{-}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}=|{\mathrm{D}}f_{j}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ on }\{x\in X:f_{-}=f_{j}\}.

Remark 2.4
.

Notice that the product in (
2.9
) is well defined since there
exists a
{\mathscr{L}}^{1}
-negligible Borel set
N\subset\mathbb{R}
such that

\phi
is differentiable in
\mathbb{R}\setminus N
and

|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}
vanishes
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. in
f^{-1}(N)
thanks to
the locality property (
2.8
).

Proof.

We give a few references for the proofs.
The case when
p=2
,
\mathscr{A}=\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
and
the local slope of
f
is used to define relaxed gradients
have been considered in
[
7
, Sec. 4]
, whose proof
generalizes easily to the case
p\in(1,\infty)
and
the asymptotic Lipschitz constant (
2.1
), see also
[
5
]
.

The definition and the properties involving a general unital
subalgebra
\mathscr{A}
have been discussed in
[
44
, Sec. 3]
:
points (1,2) correspond to Lemma 3.1.6 and Corollary 3.1.9,
(3) has been stated in Lemma 3.1.11, (4) refers to Corollary 3.1.10,
(5,6,7,8) are proved in Theorem 3.1.12 and its Corollary 3.1.13.

Let us make three further technical comments:

•

both
[
7
,
44
]
involve an auxiliary topology

\tau
:
in the present case, being
X
complete and separable
and
{\sf d}
a canonical metric (thus
{\sf d}
only take finite
values),
we can select
\tau
as the (Polish) topology induced by
{\sf d}
.

•

In order to deal with
extended
distances, in

[
44
]
has also been assumed that the
unital algebra
\mathscr{A}

satisfies the
stronger
compatibility condition

(2.12)

{\sf d}(x,y)=\sup\Big\{f(x)-f(y):f\in\mathscr{A},\ \mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(f,X)\leq 1\Big\},

which clearly implies that
\mathscr{A}
separates the points of
X
as in (
2.2
).
However, such a property is not needed in the construction and the
proofs of
Section 3.1.1 of
[
44
]
.
The only point where (
2.12
) explicitly occurs is in
the proof of Locality
[
44
, Lemma 3.1.11]
, to ensure
that the restriction of
\mathscr{A}
to each compact set
K\subset X
is
uniformly dense in
{\mathrm{C}}(K)
, a property which is guaranteed
in the present setting by
(
2.2
) thanks to Stone-Weierstrass Theorem.

•

The standard approach of
[
7
,
44
]

considers first functions
f
belonging to
L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
instead of
general
\mathfrak{m}
-measurable functions.
However, the compatibility with truncations showing that
for every
k>0

(2.13)

|{\mathrm{D}}\,T_{k}(f)|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(x)=\begin{cases}|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(x)&\text{if }|f(x)|<k,\\
0&\text{if }|f(x)|\geq k,\end{cases}\qquad T_{k}(f):=-k\lor f\land k,

and the possibility to find strong approximations of
T_{k}(f)\in L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
(recall that
\mathfrak{m}
is finite) satisfying
(
2.5
) and taking values in
[-k,k]

(see
[
44
, Cor 2.1.24, Cor. 3.1.9]
where an approximation argument involving odd polynomials is implemented)
allow for a standard extension of the theory from

L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
to

L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
, see also the discussion related to (4.16) of

[
7
]
. Notice also that, from a metric point of view, there is no reason to couple the integrability of a function
f
and the one of its minimal relaxed gradient
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}
. Also the choice of working in
L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
gives more flexibility and in particular allows to treat the distance function
{\sf d}_{y}
from one point
y\in X
without imposing any integrability condition. This will be crucial in the rest of this paper (see e.g. Theorem
2.13
).
∎

Starting from Definition
2.1
and using the
properties of Theorem
2.3
it is natural to introduce the
following notions.

Definition 2.5

(Cheeger energy and Sobolev space)
.

We call
D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A})
the set of functions in
L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
with
a
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient and we set

(2.14)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f):=\int_{X}|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}^{p}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)\quad\text{for every $f\in D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A})$},

with
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f):=+\infty
if
f\not\in D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A}).

The Sobolev space
H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A})
is defined as

L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})\cap D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A})

and
it is a Banach space with the norm

\|f\|_{H^{1,p}(X,d,\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A})}^{p}:=\|f\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f)
.
As usual, we will write
D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m}),\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f)
,
H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
and
\|f\|_{H^{1,p}}
when
\mathscr{A}=\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
.

Remark 2.6

(Cheeger energy as relaxation of the pre-Cheeger
energy)
.

We can equivalently define the Cheeger energy
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}
as a sort of

L^{0}
-lower semicontinuous relaxation of the restriction to

\mathscr{A}
of the
pre-Cheeger energy

\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}
,
the latter being defined as

(2.15)

\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f):=\int_{X}(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f)^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m},\quad f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X).

In other words, for every
f\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
it holds (
[
44
, Corollary 3.1.7]
)

(2.16)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f)=\inf\left\{\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f_{n}):f_{n}\in\mathscr{A},\,f_{n}\to f\text{ in }L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})\right\}.

In particular the functional
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}
is lower semicontinuous in

L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
.
Here the choice of the
L^{0}
-topology
does not play a crucial role, since, by Theorem
2.3
(2), the restriction of
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}
to
L^{q}(X,\mathfrak{m})
,
q\in[1,\infty)
,
can be equivalently obtained as
L^{q}
-relaxation:

(2.17)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f)=\inf\left\{\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f_{n}):f_{n}\in\mathscr{A},\,f_{n}\to f\text{ in }L^{q}(X,\mathfrak{m})\right\},\quad f\in L^{q}(X,\mathfrak{m}).

Notice also that, when
\mathfrak{m}
has not full support, two different elements
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathscr{A}
may give rise to the same equivalence class in
L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
. In this case,
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}
can be equivalently defined as the
L^{0}
-lower semicontinuous relaxation of the functional

\widetilde{\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}}_{p}(f):=\inf\left\{\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(g):g\in\mathscr{A},\,g=f\text{ $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.}\right\},\quad f\in\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{m}},

where
\mathscr{A}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is the quotient of
\mathscr{A}
with respect to equality
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e..

It is clear that we have the obvious implication for
f\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
:

(2.18)

\text{$f$ has a $(p,\mathscr{A})$-relaxed gradient}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{$f$ has a $(p,\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X))$-relaxed gradient and}\\
&|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}\leq|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\text{ $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in $X$}.\end{aligned}\right.

The converse implication
together with the identity
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}=|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}

is an important density property for an algebra
\mathscr{A}
:
by Theorem
2.3
(2), it is equivalent to the following
property.

Definition 2.7

(Density in energy of a subalgebra of Lipschitz functions)
.

We say that a subalgebra
\mathscr{A}\subset\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
is
dense in
p
-energy

if for every
f\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
with a
p
-relaxed gradient
there exists a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying

(2.19)

f_{n}\in\mathscr{A},\quad f_{n}\to f\text{ $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in $X$},\quad\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f_{n}\to|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}\text{ strongly in }L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m}).

When
\mathscr{A}
is unital and separating, this is equivalent to the fact
that
f

has a
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient and

(2.20)

|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}=|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in $X$}.

In particular
D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A})=D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
.

Remark 2.8

(Comparison with the Newtonian approach)
.

By the identification (
1.12
) when
\mathscr{A}=\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
we always
have

(2.21)

H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})=\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m}),\quad|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{N}=|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}\quad\text{for every }f\in\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m}).

If
\mathscr{A}
is dense in
p
-energy and
f\in\hat{N}^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})

we thus obtain

(2.22)

|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}=|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}=|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{N}\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in $X$}.

Notice that (
2.22
) and (
1.5
) immediately yield the
uniform upper bound in terms of the pointwise Lipschitz constant

(2.23)

\text{if $f$ is Lipschitz then}\quad|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq|{\mathrm{D}}f|\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in $X$}.

Remark 2.9
.

As we already mentioned in Remark
2.6
, the choice of
arbitrary measurable maps
f\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})

in Definition
2.7
and of the pointwise

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. convergence in (
2.19
) is not restrictive:
a simple truncation argument (which can be implemented by using odd
polynomials, see
[
44
, Corollary 2.1.24]
) shows that

\mathscr{A}
is dense in
p
-energy if and only if
for every
f\in L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
with a
p
-relaxed gradient
there exists a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying

(2.24)

f_{n}\in\mathscr{A},\quad f_{n}\to f\text{ in $L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})$},\quad\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f_{n}\to|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}\text{ strongly in }L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m}).

If
\mathscr{A}
is unital and separating this is equivalent to

H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A})=H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
with equal norms.

A first sufficient condition to obtain the density in energy of a subalgebra
\mathscr{A}
, in the more general framework of
extended topological metric measure spaces,
is provided by the
compatibility condition (
2.12
)

[
44
, Theorems 3.2.7, 5.3.1]

(see also
[
9
]

for the algebra generated by truncated distance functions).

In the present Polish setting,
we notice that
(
2.19
) (and, a fortiori, (
2.12
))
implies the weaker condition

(2.25)

for every
y\in X
the function

{\sf d}_{y}:x\mapsto{\sf d}(x,y)

has
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient
1
,

which is equivalent, thanks to Theorem
2.3
( 3), to

(2.26)

|{\mathrm{D}}{\sf d}_{y}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq 1\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in $X$}.

In fact,
using the truncations (
2.13
), each function
{\sf d}_{y}
can be approximated by the increasing sequence

f_{k}:=T_{k}{\sf d}_{y}
of bounded
1
-Lipschitz maps, so that

(2.27)

|{\mathrm{D}}{\sf d}_{y}|_{\star}\leq 1\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in $X$ for every $y\in X$},

and therefore (
2.19
) yields (
2.26
).

Remark 2.10

(The effect of truncations)
.

The
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient is not affected by truncations of
the distance functions, in particular it is not restrictive
to assume
{\sf d}
bounded above by a constant, e.g.
1
.
In fact, if we introduce a parameter
a>0
and the truncated distance

(2.28)

{\sf d}_{a}(x_{1},x_{2}):={\sf d}(x_{1},x_{2})\land a\quad\text{for every
}x_{1},x_{2}\in X,

(X,{\sf d}_{a})
is still a complete and
separable metric space, the sets
\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X,{\sf d})
and

\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X,{\sf d}_{a})
coincide, and it is easy to check that

(2.29)

\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{{\sf d}}f=\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{{\sf d}_{a}}f\quad\text{for every bounded and Lipschitz function $f$}.

We deduce that
{\sf d}
and
{\sf d}_{a}
induce the same
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed
gradient.
Notice moreover that using (
2.28
) we can also easily cover the case of
extended distances (i.e. possibly assuming the value
+\infty
),

provided
(X,{\sf d}_{a})
is a separable metric space
.
The case when
(X,{\sf d}_{a})
is not separable requires a more refined
setting involving an auxiliary topology
\tau

[
44
]
.

It is possible to express (
2.26
) in a more flexible way,
by using suitable nonlinear functions of
{\sf d}_{y}
.
We state a general result.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
I=(a,b)
be an interval (possibly unbounded) of
\mathbb{R}
and
let
\zeta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a Lipschitz and nondecreasing map satisfying

(2.30)

the restriction of
\zeta
to
I
is
of class
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
with
\zeta^{\prime}(s)>0
if
s\in I
.

If
f:X\to\overline{I}
is a Borel function, then the condition

(2.31)

f\in D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A}),\quad|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq 1

is equivalent
to

(2.32)

\zeta\circ f\in D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A}),\quad\big|{\mathrm{D}}(\zeta\circ f)\big|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(x)\leq\zeta^{\prime}(f(x))\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $x\in X$}.

Proof.

It is clear that if
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq 1
then
(
2.32
) holds, thanks to (
2.9
).
In order to prove the converse implication, we consider a strictly
decreasing sequence
a_{n}\downarrow a
, a strictly increasing
sequence
b_{n}\uparrow b

and nondecreasing and bounded Lipschitz functions
\psi_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

such that

\psi_{n}(z)=a_{n}\ \text{if }z<\zeta(a_{n}),\quad\psi_{n}(\zeta(s))=s\ \text{for every }s\in[a_{n},b_{n}],\quad\psi_{n}(z)=b_{n}\ \text{if }z>\zeta(b_{n}).

The
restriction of
\psi_{n}
to the interval
[\zeta(a_{n}),\zeta(b_{n})]
is of
class
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
.

Setting
h(x):=\zeta(f(x))
,
the Chain rule (
2.9
) yields

|{\mathrm{D}}(\psi_{n}\circ h)|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(x)\leq(\psi_{n}^{\prime}\circ h)\,|{\mathrm{D}}h|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(x)\leq(\psi_{n}^{\prime}\circ\zeta(f(x)))\zeta^{\prime}(f(x)).

Since
\psi_{n}(h(x))=a_{n}\lor f(x)\land b_{n}
,
the locality property (
2.8
), the truncation property

2.3
(8), and the fact that

\psi_{n}^{\prime}(\zeta(s))\zeta^{\prime}(s)=1
if
s\in[a_{n},b_{n}]
yield

(2.33)

|{\mathrm{D}}(\psi_{n}\circ h)|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq 1\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.}

Since
\psi_{n}\circ h\to f
pointwise in
X
as
n\to\infty
, passing to the limit
in
(
2.33
) we get
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq 1
.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

Thanks to Lemma
2.11
,
if
{\sf d}
is a bounded metric and
q>1
,
(
2.26
) is equivalent to

(2.34)

|{\mathrm{D}}{\sf d}^{q}_{y}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(x)\leq q\,{\sf d}_{y}^{q-1}(x)\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $x\in X$}.

In particular, if (
2.34
) holds for some
q\geq 1
, it
holds for any
q\geq 1
.

2.2.
A density result

We have seen that in the present setting of Polish spaces,
condition (
2.26
)
(or, equivalently, (
2.32
) for some admissible truncation
satisfying (
2.30
))
is a necessary condition for the validity of
the approximation property
(
2.19
) and of the identification
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}=|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}
.
We want to show that (
2.26
) or (
2.32
) are also

sufficient
conditions.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
be a Polish metric measure space,
let
Y\subset X
be a dense subset,
and let
\mathscr{A}
be a unital separating subalgebra of
\mathop{\rm Lip}_{b}\nolimits(X)

as in (
2.2
).
If

(2.35)

\text{for every }y\in{Y}\text{ it holds}\quad{\sf d}_{y}\in D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A}),\quad\big|{\mathrm{D}}{\sf d}_{y}\big|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq 1

then

\mathscr{A}
is dense in
p
-energy according to Definition

2.7
.

Proof.

We split the proof in various steps.
Notice that by (
2.18
)
it is sufficient to prove that

(2.36)

|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in $X$}.

(1)
It is not restrictive
to assume
{\sf d}
bounded above by
1

: see Remark
2.10
.

By Lemma
2.11
and Remark
2.12
we know that (
2.34
) holds
for every
y\in Y
and every
q\geq 1
.

(2)
It is sufficient to prove that

(2.37)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f)\leq\int_{X}(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f)^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}=\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f)\quad\text{for every $f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)$}.

In fact, if
f
has (
p,\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
)-relaxed gradient, by
(
2.5
) we can find a sequence
f_{n}\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
such that

f_{n}\to f

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. and
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f_{n}\to|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}
strongly in

L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})

as
n\to\infty.

By the
L^{0}
-lower semicontinuity of the

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}
-energy, passing to the limit in (
2.37
)
written for
f_{n}
we get

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f)=\int_{X}|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}\leq\int_{X}|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}=\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f)<\infty.

We deduce that
f
has a
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient and
that (
2.20
) holds,
since
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}\leq|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.

(3)
For every
f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
and
t>0
we introduce the Hopf-Lax
regularization
{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f:X\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

(2.38)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f(x):={}

\displaystyle\inf_{y\in X}\frac{1}{qt^{q-1}}{\sf d}^{q}(x,y)+f(y),\quad x\in X,

where
q\in(1,+\infty)
is the conjugate exponent of
p
i.e.
1/q+1/p=1
.
It is clear that
{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f
is bounded (it takes values in the
interval

[\inf_{X}f,\sup_{X}f]
) and Lipschitz, being the infimum of a family
of uniformly Lipschitz functions.
We consider the upper semicontinuous function

[
7
, (3.4) and Prop. 3.2]

(2.39)

\displaystyle{\sf D}_{t}^{+}f(x):=

\displaystyle\sup_{(y_{n})}\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\sf d}(x,y_{n}),

where the
(y_{n})_{n}
’s vary among all the minimizing sequences
of (
2.38
).

{\sf D}_{t}^{+}f
is also uniformly bounded and satisfies
(see e.g.
[
44
, Lemma 3.2.1]
)

(2.40)

\left(\frac{{\sf D}_{t}^{+}f(x)}{t}\right)^{q}\leq\left(q\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(f,X)\right)^{p}.

In fact, if
y_{n}
is a minimizing sequence of (
2.38
), for
every
\varepsilon>0
we eventually have

\frac{1}{qt^{q-1}}{\sf d}^{q}(x,y_{n})+f(y_{n})\leq{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f(x)+\varepsilon\leq f(x)+\varepsilon

i.e., setting
L:=\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(f,X)
,

\frac{1}{t^{q}}{\sf d}^{q}(x,y_{n})\leq\frac{\varepsilon q}{t}+\frac{q}{t}(f(x)-f(y_{n}))\leq\frac{\varepsilon q}{t}+qL\frac{{\sf d}(x,y_{n})}{t}\leq\frac{\varepsilon q}{t}+(qL)^{p}+\frac{{\sf d}^{q}(x,y_{n})}{qt^{q}p^{1/(p-1)}}.

We thus get

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{t^{q}}{\sf d}^{q}(x,y_{n})\leq\frac{\varepsilon q}{t}+(qL)^{p}

which yields (
2.40
) since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary.

(4)

For every
f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
and for every
t>0

(2.41)

|{\mathrm{D}}{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(x)\leq\left(t^{-1}{\sf D}_{t}^{+}f(x)\right)^{q-1}\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $x\in X$}.

Let
Y^{\prime}=\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a countable set dense in
Y
;
since
f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
it is easy to check that

(2.42)

{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f(x)=\inf_{y\in Y}\frac{1}{qt^{q-1}}{\sf d}^{q}(x,y)+f(y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{Q}}^{n}_{t}f(x),\quad{\mathsf{Q}}^{n}_{t}f(x):=\min_{1\leq k\leq n}\frac{1}{qt^{q-1}}{\sf d}^{q}(x,y_{k})+f(y_{k}).

We consider now the upper semicontinuous function

(2.43)

{\sf D}^{n}_{t}(x):=\max\Big\{{\sf d}(x,y_{k}):1\leq k\leq n,\ {\mathsf{Q}}_{t}^{n}(x)=\frac{1}{qt^{q-1}}{\sf d}^{q}(x,y_{k})+f(y_{k})\Big\}.

By (
2.34
) and Theorem
2.3
(8), we have that
(t^{-1}{\sf D}^{n}_{t})^{q-1}
is a
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient of
{\mathsf{Q}}^{n}_{t}f
.
It is then clear that for every
x
there exists a sequence

n\mapsto y^{\prime}(n,x)
with
y^{\prime}(n,x)\in\{y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}\}
such that

{\sf D}^{n}_{t}(x)={\sf d}(x,y^{\prime}(n,x))
and

{\mathsf{Q}}^{n}_{t}f(x)=\frac{1}{qt^{q-1}}{\sf d}^{q}(x,y^{\prime}(n,x))+f(y^{\prime}(n,x))\to{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f(x)

as
n\to\infty
, i.e.
y^{\prime}(n,x)
is a minimizing sequence of (
2.38
).
We deduce that

(2.44)

\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\sf D}^{n}_{t}(x)=\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\sf d}(x,y^{\prime}(n,x))\leq{\sf D}_{t}^{+}f(x)\text{ for every }x\in X.

Since
{\sf D}^{n}_{t}f
are uniformly bounded, up to extracting a
suitable subsequence we can suppose that

(t^{-1}{\sf D}^{n}_{t})^{q-1}\rightharpoonup^{*}G
weakly*
L^{\infty}(X,\mathfrak{m})
so that, by Theorem
2.3
(1),
G

is a
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient of
{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f
, hence
|{\mathrm{D}}{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq G

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. by Theorem
2.3
(3). Also notice that by Fatou’s lemma and weak*
L^{\infty}(X,\mathfrak{m})
convergence, we have

\int_{B}G\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{B}(t^{-1}{\sf D}^{n}_{t})^{q-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}\leq\int_{B}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}(t^{-1}{\sf D}^{n}_{t}(x))^{q-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)\leq\int_{B}(t^{-1}{\mathrm{D}}_{t}^{+}f(x))^{q-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x),

for every Borel set
B\subset X
. We conclude that
|{\mathrm{D}}{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq(t^{-1}{\mathrm{D}}_{t}^{+}f(x))^{q-1}
for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
x\in X
.

(5)

For every
x\in X,\ t>0,
and
f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)

we have

(2.45)

\displaystyle\frac{f(x)-{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f(x)}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{{\mathrm{D}}_{rt}^{+}f(x)}{rt}\Big)^{q}\,{\mathrm{d}}r,

(2.46)

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{f(x)-{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f(x)}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p}\big(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f(x)\big)^{p}.

This follows by
[
44
, Thm. 3.2.4]

(see also
[
4
, Thm. 3.1.4, Lemma 3.1.5]
).

( 6)

Conclusion.

We argue as in
[
44
, Theorem 3.2.7]
:
(
2.45
) and (
2.40
) yield the uniform bound

(2.47)

\frac{f(x)-{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f(x)}{t}\leq\frac{1}{p}\big(q\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(f,X)\big)^{p}\quad\text{for every }x\in X,\ t>0.

Integrating (
2.46
) in
X

and applying Fatou’s Lemma we get

(2.48)

\limsup_{t\downarrow 0}\int_{X}\frac{f(x)-{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f(x)}{t}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)\leq\frac{1}{p}\int_{X}\big(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f(x)\big)^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x).

On the other hand, (
2.45
) and Fubini’s Theorem yield

(2.49)

\int_{X}\frac{f(x)-{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f(x)}{t}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)=\frac{1}{p}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{X}\Big(\frac{{\sf D}_{rt}^{+}f(x)}{rt}\Big)^{q}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}r.

A further application of Fatou’s Lemma yields

(2.50)

\liminf_{t\downarrow 0}\int_{X}\frac{f(x)-{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f(x)}{t}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)\geq\frac{1}{p}\liminf_{t\downarrow 0}\int_{X}\Big(\frac{{\sf D}_{t}^{+}f(x)}{t}\Big)^{q}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x).

Using the fact that

t^{-1}{\sf D}_{t}^{+}f
is uniformly bounded by (
2.40
), we can
find a decreasing and vanishing sequence

n\mapsto t(n)
and a limit function
G\in L^{\infty}(X,\mathfrak{m})

such that

\displaystyle\left(t(n)^{-1}{\sf D}_{t(n)}^{+}f\right)^{q-1}\rightharpoonup^{*}G\quad\text{weakly${}^{*}$ in
$L^{\infty}(X,\mathfrak{m})$}\quad\text{as }n\to\infty,

(2.51)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}\Big(\frac{{\sf D}_{t(n)}^{+}f(x)}{t(n)}\Big)^{q}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)=\liminf_{t\downarrow 0}\int_{X}\Big(\frac{{\sf D}_{t}^{+}f(x)}{t}\Big)^{q}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x).

Since
\left(t^{-1}{\sf D}_{t}^{+}f\right)^{q-1}
is a
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient of

{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f
by claim (4) and
{\mathsf{Q}}_{t}f\to f
pointwise
everywhere, using Theorem
2.3
(1) we get that
G
is
a
(p,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient of
f
.

Using the lower semicontinuity of the

L^{p}
-norm w.r.t. the weak
∗

L^{\infty}(X,\mathfrak{m})

convergence, we get that

(2.52)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}\Bigg(\frac{{\sf D}_{t(n)}^{+}f(x)}{t(n)}\Bigg)^{q}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}\Bigg(\frac{{\sf D}_{t(n)}^{+}f(x)}{t(n)}\Bigg)^{p(q-1)}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)

(2.53)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{X}G^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)

(2.54)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{X}|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}^{p}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x),

where we also used the pointwise minimality of
|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}
given by Theorem
2.3
(3).
Combining (
2.52
), (
2.51
), (
2.50
) and
(
2.48
)
we deduce that

\int_{X}|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}^{p}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)\leq\int_{X}\big(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f(x)\big)^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)

so that (
2.37
) holds.
∎

Corollary 2.14

(Density in energy of
\mathscr{A}
in
H^{1,p}
)
.

If
\mathscr{A}
is a separating unital subalgebra of
\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
satisfying
(
2.35
)
then

(2.55)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f)=\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f)=\mathsf{N\kern-2.5ptE}_{p}(f)\quad\text{for every $\mathfrak{m}$-measurable function $f:X\to\mathbb{R}$.}

In particular,
H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})=H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A})
.

As we have already said, (
2.55
) can be interpreted as a density
result in
H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
: for every
f\in H^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})

there exists a sequence
f_{n}\in\mathscr{A}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

(2.56)

f_{n}\to f,\ \mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f_{n}\to|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{*}\quad\text{strongly in }L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m}),\quad\int_{X}|\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f_{n}|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}\to\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f)\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

2.3.
Applications

We first recall a useful result showing that it is possible to remove the assumption that

\mathscr{A}
is unital, if
\mathscr{A}
satisfies a suitable tightness condition.
We will denote by
\mathbbm{1}
the unit constant function.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
\mathscr{A}\subset\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
be a separating subalgebra of Lipschitz
functions
and let

(2.57)

\mathscr{A}_{1}:=\mathscr{A}\oplus\{c\mathbbm{1}\}=\Big\{f+c\mathbbm{1}:f\in\mathscr{A},\,c\in\mathbb{R}\Big\}

be the minimal unital subalgebra containing
\mathscr{A}
.
If
\mathscr{A}_{1}
is dense in
p
-energy and
there exist a sequences of compact sets
K_{n}\subset X
and functions

f_{n}\in\mathscr{A}
such that

(2.58)

f_{n}(x)\geq 1\ \text{for every $x\in K_{n}$},\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{X\setminus K_{n}}\Big(1+|\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f_{n}(x)|^{p}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)=0,

then
\mathscr{A}
is dense in
p
-energy as well.

The
proof
is a simple adaptation of
[
44
, Proposition
5.3.2]
.
The next result shows that the algebra
generated
by (suitable compositions/truncations of) distance functions
is always sufficient to generate the Sobolev space
H^{1,2}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
.

Theorem 2.16
.

Let
Y
be a dense subset of
X

and
let
\zeta:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
be a Lipschitz nondecreasing
function
such that
\zeta^{\prime}>0
in an interval
I=(0,r)\subset(0,+\infty)
and
\zeta\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(I)
.
Then the unital algebra
\mathscr{A}
generated by the functions
x\mapsto\zeta({\sf d}(x,y))

is dense in
p
-energy.

Proof.

Thanks to Remark
2.10
, we can assume that
{\sf d}
is bounded above by
r
.
It is not difficult to check that
\mathscr{A}
separates the points of

X
, so that
in order to apply Theorem
2.13
, it is enough to check
that (
2.32
) with
f:={\sf d}_{y}
(recall the notation (
2.25
)) holds.

Such a property follows immediately from the corresponding estimate
on the asymptotic Lipschitz constant: for every
y\in Y
and

g(x):=\zeta({\sf d}_{y}(x))
,
a simple direct computation shows that

\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits g(x)\leq\zeta^{\prime}({\sf d}_{y}(x))\quad\text{for every }x\in{\mathrm{B}}(y,r).

Since
g\in\mathscr{A}
we have
|{\mathrm{D}}g|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits g\leq\zeta^{\prime}(f)
, so that (
2.32
) holds and we conclude by applying Lemma
2.11
.
∎

We now consider a simple application
of Theorem
2.13
to the
case when
p=2
and
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f
has good properties for functions of

\mathscr{A}
.

Theorem 2.17

(A Hilbertianity condition)
.

Let
p=2
and let
\mathscr{A}
be a separating unital subalgebra of

\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X)
satisfying (
2.35
).
If for every
f,g\in\mathscr{A}

(2.59)

\int_{X}\Big(|\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits(f+g)|^{2}+|\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits(f-g)|^{2}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}=2\int_{X}\Big(|\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f|^{2}+|\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits g|^{2}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m},

then
H^{1,2}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
is a Hilbert space,
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
is a Dirichlet (thus quadratic)
form, and

\mathscr{A}
is strongly dense.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove that
the Cheeger energy is a quadratic form in its domain.
Thanks to
[
16
, Prop. 11.9]
and the
2
-homogeneity of

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
,
this property is equivalent
to

(2.60)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(f+g)+\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(f-g)\leq 2\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(f)+2\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(g)\quad\text{for every }f,g\in H^{1,2}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m}).

We can find two sequences
f_{n},g_{n}\in\mathscr{A}
such that

f_{n}\to f,\ g_{n}\to g
in
\mathfrak{m}
-measure as
n\to\infty
and

\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f_{n}\to|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}
,
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits g_{n}\to|{\mathrm{D}}g|_{\star}
in

L^{2}(X,\mathfrak{m})
.
Clearly we have
f_{n}+g_{n}\to f+g
,
f_{n}-g_{n}\to f-g
in
\mathfrak{m}
-measure
and (
2.59
) shows that
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits(f_{n}+g_{n})
and
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits(f_{n}-g_{n})
are
uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(X,\mathfrak{m})
.
Up to extracting a suitable sequence, it is not restrictive to
assume that
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits(f_{n}+g_{n})\rightharpoonup G_{+}\geq|{\mathrm{D}}(f+g)|_{\star}
and

\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits(f_{n}-g_{n})\rightharpoonup G_{-}\geq|{\mathrm{D}}(f-g)|_{\star}

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. in
X
.
(
2.59
) then yields

\displaystyle\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(f+g)+\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(f-g)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}|{\mathrm{D}}(f+g)|_{\star}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}+\int_{X}|{\mathrm{D}}(f-g)|_{\star}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}|\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits(f_{n}+g_{n})|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}+\int_{X}|\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits(f_{n}-g_{n})|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{n\to\infty}2\int_{X}|\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits f_{n}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}+2\int_{X}|\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits g_{n}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}=2\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(f)+\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(g).

Since
H^{1,2}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
is Banach space,
we deduce that
H^{1,2}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
is a Hilbert space, so it is
reflexive.
This also shows that
\mathscr{A}
is strongly dense.
∎

Remark 2.18
.

In the framework of Theorem
2.17
, there exists a scalar product
{\langle}\cdot,\cdot{\rangle}_{H^{1,2}}
on
H^{1,2}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
inducing the norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1,2}}
and satisfying

(2.61)

{\langle}f,g{\rangle}_{H^{1,2}}=\int_{X}fg\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}+\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(f,g)\text{ for every }f,g\in H^{1,2}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m}),

where
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the bilinear form associated to

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(\cdot)
.

Remark 2.19
.

If (
2.59
) holds then the restriction
(\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2},\mathscr{A})
of
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
to
\mathscr{A}

is
a quadratic form which is induced by a corresponding bilinear form

\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)
defined by the parallelogram rule.
We recall that such a form is
closable
(see e.g.
[
13
, §1.3]
,
[
32
, Chapter I, §.3]
) if for any sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathscr{A}

(2.62)

f_{n}\to 0\quad\text{in }L^{2}(X,\mathfrak{m}),\quad\limsup_{m,n\to\infty}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(f_{n}-f_{m})=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(f_{n})=0.

Theorem
2.17
shows in particular that if

(\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2},\mathscr{A})
is quadratic and closable,
then the Cheeger energy
(\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2},H^{1,2}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m}))
coincides with the smallest closed
extension of
(\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2},\mathscr{A})
. In this case, trivially, the restriction of

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
to
\mathscr{A}
coincides with
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
.
Since the Cheeger energy
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
is quasi-regular (see

[
8
, Lemma 6.7]
,
[
43
, Thm. 4.1]
,

[
19
, Prop. 3.21]
,

[
42
]
), as a by-product we obtain the quasi-regularity of
the closure of
(\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2},\mathscr{A})
.

An immediate consequence is the Hilbertianity of

H^{1,2}(\mathbb{H},{\sf d}_{\mathbb{H}},\mathfrak{m})
in the case when
(\mathbb{H},{\sf d}_{\mathbb{H}})
is a
separable Hilbert space (in particular
\mathbb{R}^{d}
) endowed with the
distance induced by its Hilbertian norm
[
20
,
21
,
44
]
.

Corollary 2.20
.

Let
(\mathbb{H},{\sf d}_{\mathbb{H}})
be a separable Hilbert space and let
\mathfrak{m}
be a
finite and positive Borel measure on
\mathbb{H}
.
Then
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{H},{\sf d}_{\mathbb{H}},\mathfrak{m})
is a Hilbert space.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be the algebra
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{H})
of bounded
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}

functions with bounded continuous gradient. It is
immediate to check that for every
\phi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{H})
we have

\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits\phi(x)=\|\nabla\phi(x)\|_{\mathbb{H}}
so that
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
is a quadratic
form on
\mathscr{A}
, thus satisfying (
2.59
).

On the other hand
\mathscr{A}
contains the functions

x\mapsto\tanh({\sf d}^{2}(x,y))
,
y\in\mathbb{H}
, so that we can apply
Theorem
2.16
.
∎

Remark 2.21

(Density of
{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
)
.

When
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
is finite dimensional,
we can also prove that the algebra
\mathscr{A}={\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is strongly dense in
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
.
In fact, if
\zeta
is the restriction to
[0,\infty)
of a smooth
nondecreasing
transition function
\tilde{\zeta}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying
\tilde{\zeta}(s)=0
if
s\leq 0
,

\tilde{\zeta}(s)=1
is
s>1
and
\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}(s)>0
if
s\in(0,1)
,
it is immediate to check that for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the functions

\tilde{\zeta}({\sf d}_{y})
belong to
\mathscr{A}_{1}
, so that
\mathscr{A}_{1}
is dense in

2
-energy by Theorem
2.16
.

On the other hand, being
\mathfrak{m}
tight, it is easy to check that
\mathscr{A}

satisfies (
2.58
), so that we can apply Proposition
2.15
.

2.4.
Intrinsic distances

By using the general properties of metric Sobolev spaces
and the equivalence with the Newtonian viewpoint based on the
notion of upper gradient
[
12
,
28
]
it is
possible to improve considerably the density result of Corollary

2.14
.
Let us first recall the notion of
metric velocity

(2.63)

|\dot{\gamma}|_{\sf d}(t):=\limsup_{h\to 0}\frac{{\sf d}(\gamma(t+h),\gamma(t))}{|h|}

and
length

(2.64)

\displaystyle\ell_{{\sf d}}(\gamma,[\alpha,\beta]):={}

\displaystyle\sup\Big\{\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\sf d}(\gamma(t_{n-1}),\gamma(t_{n})):t_{0}=\alpha<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{N-1}<t_{N}=\beta\Big\}

\displaystyle=\int_{\alpha}^{\beta}|\dot{\gamma}|_{\sf d}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

of a

{\sf d}
-Lipschitz curve
\gamma:[a,b]\to X
; here
[\alpha,\beta]\subset[a,b]
and we just write
\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma)
for

\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma,[a,b])
.

If
Y\subset X
is a given set, we can introduce the
length (or intrinsic) extended
distance

{\sf d}_{Y,\ell}
induced by
{\sf d}
on
Y
, as the infimum of the
length of
Y
-valued Lipschitz curves connecting two given points

y_{0},y_{1}\in Y
:

(2.65)

\displaystyle{\sf d}_{Y,\ell}(y_{0},y_{1}):={}

\displaystyle\inf\Big\{\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma):\gamma\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits([0,1];(Y,{\sf d})),\ \gamma(0)=y_{0},\ \gamma(1)=y_{1}\Big\}

(2.66)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\inf\Big\{\ell>0:\gamma\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits([0,\ell];(Y,{\sf d})),\ \gamma(0)=y_{0},\ \gamma(\ell)=y_{1},\ |\dot{\gamma}|_{\sf d}\leq 1\text{ a.e.}\Big\}.

Clearly we have

(2.67)

{\sf d}(y_{0},y_{1})\leq{\sf d}_{X,\ell}(y_{0},y_{1})\leq{\sf d}_{Y,\ell}(y_{0},y_{1})\quad\text{for every }y_{0},y_{1}\in Y.

If
g:X\to[0,+\infty]

is a Borel function, the integral of
g
along
\gamma
is defined by

(2.68)

\int_{\gamma}g:=\int_{a}^{b}g(\gamma(t))|\dot{\gamma}|_{\sf d}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t.

It is well known that length and integral are invariant with respect
to arc-length reparametrization of
\gamma
and it is always possible to find
a
1
-Lipschitz curve
R_{\gamma}:[0,\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma)]\to X
such
that

(2.69)

R_{\gamma}(\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma,[a,t]))=\gamma(t)\text{ for every }t\in[a,b],\quad|\dot{R}_{\gamma}|(s)=1\text{ a.e.\penalty\ in $[0,\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma)]$},\quad\int_{R_{\gamma}}g=\int_{\gamma}g

for every nonnegative Borel function
g
(see e.g.
[
44
, Section 3.3]
).
A Borel function
g:X\to[0,+\infty]
is an upper gradient of

f:X\to\mathbb{R}
if

(2.70)

|f(\gamma(b))-f(\gamma(a))|\leq\int_{\gamma}g\quad\text{for every $\gamma\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits([a,b];(X,{\sf d}))$.}

Functions in
\mathcal{L}^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
which admit an upper gradient in
\mathcal{L}^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})

characterize the Newtonian Sobolev space
N^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})

[
12
,
28
]
.
We state here a useful consequence of the main equivalence results

[
7
, Theorem 6.2]

[
5
, Theorem 7.4]
.

Theorem 2.22
.

Let
Y
be a
Borel subset of
X
of full
\mathfrak{m}
-measure (i.e.
\mathfrak{m}(X\setminus Y)=0
) satisfying

(2.71)

\gamma\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits([a,b];(X,{\sf d})),\quad R_{\gamma}(s)\in Y\ \text{for ${\mathscr{L}}^{1}$-a.e.\penalty\ $s\in[0,\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma)]$}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\gamma([a,b])\subset Y,

let
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
be a
\mathfrak{m}
-measurable function and let

g:Y\to[0,+\infty]
be a Borel function satisfying

(2.72)

|f(\gamma(b))-f(\gamma(a))|\leq\int_{\gamma}g\quad\text{for every $\gamma\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits([a,b];(Y,{\sf d}))$}.

If
\displaystyle\int_{Y}|g|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}<\infty
then
f
has a
p
-relaxed gradient and

(2.73)

|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}\leq g\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in }Y.

Notice that condition (
2.72
) is weaker than (
2.70
),
since the upper gradient condition is imposed only along curves taking
values in
Y
; however, starting from any
function
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(Y,\mathfrak{m})
satisfying (
2.72
) we
can define a new Borel function
\tilde{g}:X\to[0,+\infty]
whose
restriction to
Y
coincides with
g
such that
\tilde{g}\lower 3.0pt\hbox{$|_{X\setminus Y}$}\equiv+\infty
. Clearly

\int_{X}\tilde{g}^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}=\int_{Y}g^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}<+\infty\quad\text{since }\mathfrak{m}(X\setminus Y)=0.

Moreover
\tilde{g}
is an upper gradient for
f

according to (
2.70
): in fact it is sufficient to check
(
2.70
) for those curves
\gamma
with
\gamma=R_{\gamma}
and
\displaystyle\int_{\gamma}\tilde{g}<+\infty
; since
\tilde{g}(\gamma(s))=+\infty

if
\gamma(s)\not\in Y
, we deduce that
\gamma(s)\in Y
for

{\mathscr{L}}^{1}
-a.e.
s\in[0,\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma)]
so that
\gamma\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits([0,\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma)];(Y,{\sf d}))
by (
2.71
),
and (
2.70
) then follows by (
2.72
).

It is also immediate to check that (
2.71
) holds if
Y
is closed.

We consider the situation where

(A)

Y\subset X
is a Borel set with full
\mathfrak{m}
-measure
satisfying (
2.71
);

(B)

a metric

{\delta}:Y\times Y\to[0,+\infty)

is given on
Y
such that
(Y,{\delta})
is complete and separable
and
(recall Remark
2.10
)

(2.74)

{\sf d}_{1}(y_{1},y_{2})\leq{\delta}(y_{1},y_{2})\leq{\sf d}_{Y,\ell}(y_{1},y_{2})\quad\text{for every }y_{1},y_{2}\in Y.

Remark 2.23

(
Y
-intrinsic distance)
.

\delta
is intrinsically equivalent to
{\sf d}
on
Y
, i.e. every

{\sf d}
-Lipschitz curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to Y
is also

{\delta}
-Lipschitz, its
{\delta}
-length
coincides with the corresponding
{\sf d}
-length, and integration
along
\gamma
does not depend on the choice of the distance. In particular condition (
2.72
) can be equivalently stated
in terms of
{\delta}
.

To see that these conditions are implied by (
2.74
), let us fix a
{\sf d}
-Lipschitz curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to Y
with Lipschitz constant bounded by
L\geq 0
; then

{\sf d}_{Y,\ell}(\gamma(s),\gamma(t))\leq\ell_{\sf d}\left(\gamma|_{[s,t]}\right)=\int_{s}^{t}|\dot{\gamma}|_{\sf d}(r)\,{\mathrm{d}}r\leq L|t-s|\quad 0\leq s\leq t\leq 1,

so that
\gamma
is
{\sf d}_{Y,\ell}
-Lipschitz continuous and thus, by (
2.74
), also
{\delta}
-Lipschitz continuous. To see that the
{\delta}
and the
{\sf d}
-lengths of
\gamma
coincide, it is enough to show that
\ell_{\delta}(\gamma)\leq\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma)
, since (
2.74
) and the trivial equality
\ell_{{\sf d}_{1}}(\gamma)=\ell_{{\sf d}}(\gamma)
already give the other inequality; by
(
2.74
)
we immediately have
\ell_{{\delta}}(\gamma)\leq\ell_{{\sf d}_{Y,\ell}}(\gamma)
and by the very definition of
{\sf d}_{Y,\ell}
we see that
\ell_{{\sf d}_{Y,\ell}}(\gamma)\leq\ell_{{\sf d}}(\gamma)
. Finally, to see that the integral along
\gamma
does not depend on the choice of the distance, it is enough to see that
|\dot{\gamma}|_{\sf d}=|\dot{\gamma}|_{\delta}
a.e. in
[0,1]
. The
\leq
inequality is an immediate consequence of (
2.74
) and (
2.63
), while the
\geq
follows by

\frac{\delta(\gamma(s),\gamma(t))}{t-s}\leq\frac{\ell_{\delta}(\gamma|_{[s,t]})}{t-s}=\frac{\ell_{\sf d}(\gamma|_{[s,t]})}{t-s}=\frac{1}{t-s}\int_{s}^{t}|\dot{\gamma}|_{\sf d}(r)\,{\mathrm{d}}r\quad 0\leq s<t\leq 1,

and passing to the limit as
s\to t
for every Lebesgue point
t
of
|\dot{\gamma}|_{\sf d}
.

Since
\mathfrak{m}(X\setminus Y)=0

we can identify
L^{p}(Y,\mathfrak{m})
with
L^{p}(X,\mathfrak{m})
.
In general, the topology induced by
{\delta}
is finer than the
{\sf d}

topology on
Y
, and they coincide if
{\delta}
is continuous
w.r.t.
{\sf d}
.
It is also clear from property (B) that the restriction to
Y
of every
bounded
{\sf d}
-Lipschitz function

f:X\to\mathbb{R}
is also
{\delta}
-Lipschitz.
Thanks to (
2.74
)
(which in particular implies that

\delta
-balls of radius
r<1
centered at some point
y\in Y
are included in
{\sf d}
-balls of the
same radius and with the same center)
it is also clear that

(2.75)

\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{{\delta}}f(y)\leq\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{\sf d}f(y)\quad\text{for every }y\in Y,\ f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X,{\sf d}).

Since
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{{\delta}}f
is bounded and
\delta
-u.s.c. in
Y
,
it is
\mathfrak{m}
-measurable and
we can define the
{\delta}
pre-Cheeger energy

(2.76)

\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,{\delta}}(f):=\int_{Y}|\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{{\delta}}f(y)|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(y)

and we can still consider its l.s.c. envelope in
L^{0}(Y,\mathfrak{m})

(2.77)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,{\delta},\mathscr{A}}(f):=\inf\Big\{\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,{\delta}}(f_{n}):f_{n}\in\mathscr{A},\ f_{n}\to f\ \text{in }L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})\Big\}.

Theorem 2.24
.

Let
\mathscr{A}(X,{\sf d}):=\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X,{\sf d})
, let
\mathscr{A}
be a separating unital subalgebra of

\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X,{\sf d})
satisfying (
2.35
)
and assume that
(Y,\delta)
satisfies the conditions
(A), (B)
above. Then
we have

(2.78)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,{\delta},\mathscr{A}(X,{\sf d})}(f)=\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,{\delta},\mathscr{A}}(f)=\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f)=\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f)\quad\text{ for every }f\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m}).

In particular, the minimal
p
-relaxed gradients of
f\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
computed w.r.t.
(\delta,\mathscr{A})
,

(\delta,\mathop{\rm Lip}_{b}\nolimits(Y))
,

({\sf d},\mathscr{A})
or
({\sf d},\mathop{\rm Lip}_{b}\nolimits(X))
coincide and we have

D^{1,p}(Y,{\delta},\mathfrak{m})=D^{1,p}(Y,{\delta},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A})=D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})=D^{1,p}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m};\mathscr{A}).

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,{\delta}}(f)\leq\int_{X}\big(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{\sf d}f(x)\big)^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}
for every
f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X,{\sf d})
, we clearly have

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,{\delta},\mathscr{A}(X,{\sf d})}(f)\leq\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,{\delta},\mathscr{A}}(f)\leq\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,\mathscr{A}}(f)=\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f)\quad\text{for
every $f\in L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})$},

where the last equality follows from Corollary
2.14
. It is then sufficient to prove that
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p,{\delta},\mathscr{A}(X,{\sf d})}(f)\geq\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f)
in
order to get (
2.78
). Using (
2.77
) and the
L^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m})
-lower
semicontinuity of
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}

(see Remark
2.6
), the latter inequality will be a consequence of

(2.79)

\int_{Y}|\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{{\delta}}f(y)|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(y)\geq\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{p}(f)\quad\text{for
every }f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X,{\sf d}).

In order to prove (
2.79
) it is sufficient to apply
Theorem
2.22
and prove that the Borel function

g:=\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{{\delta}}f

satisfies (
2.72
).
Now we use the fact that the restriction to
Y
of a function
f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(X,{\sf d})
belongs to
\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(Y,{\delta})

and every
{\sf d}
-Lipschitz curve
\gamma
with values in
Y
is also

{\delta}
-Lipschitz, the respective lengths coincide and therefore
also the arc-length reparametrizations are the same.
Since
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{\delta}
is an upper gradient we thus obtain

|f(\gamma(b))-f(\gamma(a))|\leq\int_{\gamma}\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{\delta}f\quad\text{ for every }\gamma\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits([a,b];(Y,\delta)).\qed

By Theorem
2.22
and also using that
\mathfrak{m}(X\setminus Y)=0
, we conclude.

Combining Theorem
2.24
with Corollary

2.20
we recover the following result of
[
31
]
.

Corollary 2.25
.

Let
(\mathbb{M},{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}})
be a complete Riemannian manifold endowed with
the canonical Riemannian distance and let
\mathfrak{m}
be a
finite and positive Borel measure on
\mathbb{M}
.
Then
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{M},{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}},\mathfrak{m})
is a Hilbert space and

{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{M})
is dense in
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{M},{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}},\mathfrak{m})
.

Proof.

By Nash isometric embedding Theorem
[
36
]

we can find a dimension
d
,
and an isometric embedding

\jmath:\mathbb{M}\to\jmath(\mathbb{M})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Since
\mathbb{M}
is complete and
\jmath
is an
imbedding,
M:=\jmath(\mathbb{M})
is a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the (Riemannian) metric
{\sf d}_{M}
inherited by
{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}
given by
{\sf d}_{M}(\jmath(x),\jmath(y)):={\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}(x,y)
is an isometry. In particular
{\sf d}_{M}
induces on
M
the relative topology of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
(M,{\sf d}_{M})
is a complete and separable metric space.
Setting
\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}:=\jmath_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}
,
it is clear that the map
\jmath^{*}:f\to f\circ\jmath

is a linear isometric isomorphism between

H^{1,2}(M,{\sf d}_{M},\tilde{\mathfrak{m}})

and
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{M},{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}},\mathfrak{m})
.
It is then sufficient to prove the statement for
H^{1,2}(M,{\sf d}_{M},\tilde{\mathfrak{m}})
.

We can now apply Theorem
2.24
with
the choices
(Y,{\delta}):=(M,{\sf d}_{M})
and
X=\mathbb{R}^{d}
endowed with the
Euclidean distance
{\sf d}
.
Condition (A) clearly holds since
M
is closed in

\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}
is supported on
M
. Similarly, also (B) holds since
\jmath
is an isometric
immersion.

Remark
2.21
shows that
{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is
dense in
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},{\sf d}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}},\tilde{\mathfrak{m}})
so that

\jmath^{*}\big({\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)\subset{\mathrm{C}}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{M})

is dense in
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{M},{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}},\mathfrak{m}).

∎

3.
Wasserstein spaces

In this section we list some properties of Wasserstein spaces we will use in the sequel. A complete account of this matter can be found e.g. in
[
49
,
4
]
.

If
(X,{\sf d})
is a complete and separable metric space, we denote by

\mathcal{P}(X)
the space of Borel probability measures on
X
and by

\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
,
the set

\mathcal{P}_{2}(X):=\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)\mid\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,x_{0}){\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)<+\infty\text{ for some }x_{0}\in X\right\}.

Given
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(X)
the set of transport plans between
\mu
and
\nu
is denoted by
\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
and defined as

\Gamma(\mu,\nu):=\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}\in\mathcal{P}(X\times X)\mid\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}=\mu,\,\pi^{2}_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}=\nu\right\},

where
\pi^{i}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{i}
for every
(x_{1},x_{2})\in X\times X
and
\sharp
denotes the push forward operator.
The
L^{2}
-Wasserstein distance
W_{2}
between
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
is defined as

W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu):=\inf\left\{\int_{X\times X}{\sf d}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}\mid{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)\right\}.

It is well known that the infimum above is attained in a non-empty and
convex set
\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)\subset\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
; elements of

\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)
are called optimal transport plans.

The space
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(X),W_{2})
is complete and separable and its topology is stronger than the narrow topology, the latter being defined as the coarsest topology on
\mathcal{P}(X)
making the maps

\mu\mapsto\int_{X}\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu

continuous for every
\varphi\in{\mathrm{C}}_{b}(X)
, the space of continuous and bounded functions on
X
. In particular, for a sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
and a point
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
, we have

(3.1)

W_{2}(\mu_{n},\mu)\to 0\Leftrightarrow\begin{cases}&\displaystyle\int_{X}{\sf d}^{2}(x,x_{0}){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{n}(x)\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\sf d}^{2}(x,x_{0}){\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\quad\text{ for some }x_{0}\in X,\\
&\mu_{n}\to\mu\quad\text{narrowly in }\mathcal{P}(X).\end{cases}

Moreover, the Wasserstein distance is narrowly lower semicontinuous,
meaning that, if
(\mu_{n})_{n}
and
(\mu^{\prime}_{n})_{n}
are two sequences in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
,
\mu,,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
and
\mu_{n}\to\mu
,
\mu^{\prime}_{n}\to\mu^{\prime}

narrowly in
\mathcal{P}(X)
, then we have

\liminf_{n\to\infty}W_{2}(\mu_{n},\mu^{\prime}_{n})\geq W_{2}(\mu,\mu^{\prime}).

The following Theorem is
[
4
, Theorem 8.3.1, Proposition 8.4.5 and
Proposition 8.4.6]
in case
X=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Recall that for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

(3.2)

\Tan_{\mu}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):={}\overline{\{\nabla\varphi\mid\varphi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}}^{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Theorem 3.1

(Wasserstein velocity field)
.

Let
(\mu_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{J}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a locally absolutely
continuous curve defined in an open interval
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathbb{R}
.
There exists a Borel vector field

v:\mathcal{J}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

and
a set
A((\mu_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{J}})\subset\mathcal{J}
with

{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(\mathcal{J}\setminus A((\mu_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{J}}))=0
such that
for every
t\in A((\mu_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{J}})

\begin{gathered}v_{t}\in\Tan_{\mu_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v_{t}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}=|\dot{\mu}_{t}|^{2}=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t+h},\mu_{t})}{h^{2}},\end{gathered}

and the continuity equation

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\nabla\cdot(v_{t}\mu_{t})=0

holds in the sense of distributions in
\mathcal{J}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Moreover,
v_{t}
is uniquely determined
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu_{t};\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
t\in A((\mu_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{J}})
and

(3.3)

\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{W_{2}(({\mbox{\boldmath$i$}}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+hv_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{t},\mu_{t+h})}{|h|}=0\quad\text{for every }t\in A((\mu_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{J}}),

where
{\mbox{\boldmath$i$}}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is the identity map on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

3.1.
Kantorovich duality and estimates for Kantorovich
potentials

The Kantorovich duality for the Wasserstein distance states that

(3.4)

W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)=\sup\left\{\int_{X}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu+\int_{X}v\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu\mid(u,v)\in\text{Adm}_{2}(X)\right\}\quad\text{ for every }\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X),

where
\text{Adm}_{2}(X)
is the set of pairs
(u,v)\in{\mathrm{C}}_{b}(X)\times{\mathrm{C}}_{b}(X)
such that

u(x)+v(y)\leq{\sf d}^{2}(x,y)\quad\text{ for every }x,y\in X.

It is easy to check that for every
f\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(X,{\sf d})

(3.5)

\int_{X}f\,{\mathrm{d}}(\mu-\nu)\leq\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(f,X)W_{2}(\mu,\nu),

since choosing
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)
and setting
