L:=\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(f,X)
,

\displaystyle\int_{X}f\,{\mathrm{d}}(\mu-\nu)=\int(f(x)-f(y))\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}(x,y)\leq L\int{\sf d}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}\leq L\Big(\int{\sf d}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}\Big)^{1/2}=LW_{2}(\mu,\nu).

When
X=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the subset of

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of probability measures that are absolutely continuous
w.r.t. the
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure.
We also set

(3.6)

\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\delta_{0}).

The next result uses the celebrated Brenier-Knott-Smith Theorem

[
48
, Section 3]
to collect various useful properties of the optimal
potentials realizing the supremum in (
3.4
) in a particular geometric
situation. We will use the elementary property
that

(3.7)

\text{if $u:\overline{B(0,R)}\to[-\infty,+\infty)$ is concave with $u(0)>-\infty$
then}\quad\sup_{\overline{{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}}u=\sup_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}u,

which follows by the fact that for every
y_{0}\in\partial{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)

the concavity of
t\mapsto u(ty_{0})
in
[0,1]

yields
u(y_{0})\leq\sup_{0\leq t<1}u(ty_{0})
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

with
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\nu}=\overline{{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}
for some
R>0
. Then there
exists a unique pair of
continuous
and convex functions

(3.8)

\varphi=\Phi(\nu,\mu):{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)\to\mathbb{R},\quad\varphi^{*}=\Phi^{*}(\nu,\mu):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

such that

(i)

\varphi^{*}
is
R
-Lipschitz and

(3.9)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\varphi^{*}(y)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\varphi(x)

\displaystyle\text{for every $y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$},

(3.10)

\displaystyle\varphi(x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}y,x{\rangle}-\varphi^{*}(y)

\displaystyle\text{for every }x\in{\mathrm{B}}(0,R),

(ii)

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}(0)=\inf_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}\varphi=0
,

(iii)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi^{*}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\nu)+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu)-\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\nu,\mu)
.

Moreover the pair
(\varphi,\varphi^{*})
satisfies

(3.11)

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)=\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}\left|x-\nabla\varphi(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\nu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|y-\nabla\varphi^{*}(y)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y).

Proof.

Let us set
D:={\mathrm{B}}(0,R)
.
We know (see e.g.
[
48
, Theorem 2.9, Lemma 2.10]
)
that there exists a pair
(\phi,\phi^{*})
of lower semicontinuous proper conjugate functions such that
\phi\in L^{1}(\overline{D},\nu;(-\infty,+\infty])
,
\phi^{*}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu;(-\infty,+\infty])
and it holds

(3.12)

\int_{\overline{D}}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi^{*}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\nu)+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu)-\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\nu,\mu),

where

(3.13)

\phi^{*}(y):=\sup_{x\in\overline{D}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\phi(x)=\max_{x\in\overline{D}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\phi(x).

Recalling that
\phi
is bounded from below by an affine mapping
(and thus it is uniformly bounded from below in
\overline{D}
)
we immediately see that
\phi^{*}
takes values in
\mathbb{R}
and it is

R
-Lipschitz. Up to adding a suitable constant to
\phi
we can
also suppose that

\phi^{*}(0)=0
.

We want to show that the restriction
\varphi
of
\phi
to
{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)

combined with
\phi^{*}

satisfies conditions (i), (ii), and (iii).

(i)
:
Since
\int_{\overline{D}}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu<+\infty
and

\nu
has full support, we deduce that the proper
domain of
\phi

\{x\in\overline{D}:\phi(x)<+\infty\}
is dense in

\overline{D}
;
since the proper domain of a l.s.c. and convex function is convex
and contains the interior of its closure, we deduce that

\phi(x)<+\infty
for every
x\in D
and
\varphi:=\phi\lower 3.0pt\hbox{$|_{D}$}
is continuous in
D
.

(
3.7
) shows that the supremum defining
\phi^{*}
in (
3.13
)
can be restricted
to
D

(3.14)

\phi^{*}(y)=\sup_{x\in D}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\varphi(x),

so that (
3.9
) holds.
(
3.10
) is just an application of Fenchel-Moreau Theorem
\phi=\phi^{**}
.

(ii)
: simply follows by (
3.9
) and the fact that
\phi^{*}(0)=0
.

(iii)

It is sufficient to notice that the first integral in (
3.12
) can be restricted to
D

since
\nu(\partial D)=0
.
The equality (
3.11
) follows by
[
48
, Theorem 2.12]
.

Let us show that points (i)-(iii) are also sufficient to get
uniqueness. If
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{0}^{*})
is another pair as in the
statement satisfying points (i)-(iii), then
[
48
, Theorem
2.12]
yields
that both
\nabla\varphi
and
\nabla\varphi_{0}
are optimal
transport maps from
\nu
to
\mu
, implying that
\nabla\varphi_{0}=\nabla\varphi

{\mathscr{L}}^{d}
-a.e. in
{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)
by the
a.e. uniqueness of the optimal transport map.
Since
\inf_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}\varphi=\inf_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}\varphi_{0}=0
by

(ii)
, we get that
\varphi=\varphi_{0}
in
{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)

and therefore
\varphi^{*}=\varphi^{*}_{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by (
3.9
).
∎

The next Lemma collects useful estimates on convex functions; we set

\omega_{d}:={\mathscr{L}}^{d}\big({\mathrm{B}}(0,1)\big)
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
R,I>0
and let

\varphi:{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)\to\mathbb{R}
,
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, be two
(continuous and) convex functions satisfying

(3.15)

\begin{gathered}|\psi(y)|\leq R|y|\quad\text{for every }y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\varphi(x)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\psi(y)\ \text{for every }x\in{\mathrm{B}}(0,R),\quad\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq I.\end{gathered}

Then

\varphi
is nonnegative and satisfy the uniform bounds

(3.16)

\sup_{|x|\leq r}\varphi(x)\leq\frac{I}{\omega_{d}(R-r)^{d}},\quad\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits\big(\varphi,\overline{{\mathrm{B}}(0,r)}\big)\leq\frac{2^{d+1}I}{\omega_{d}(R-r)^{d+1}}\quad 0<r<R,

and

(3.17)

\text{$\psi$ is $R$-Lipschitz,}\quad\psi(y)=\sup_{x\in{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}{\langle}y,x{\rangle}-\varphi(x)\quad\text{for every }y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Proof.

Notice that
\psi(0)=0
yields
\varphi\geq 0
;
the integral estimate of (
3.15
) and Jensen inequality yield
for every
x\in{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)
with
\varrho:=R-|x|

(3.18)

\varphi(x)\leq\frac{1}{\omega_{d}\varrho^{d}}\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(x,\varrho)}\varphi(z)\,{\mathrm{d}}z\leq\frac{I}{\omega_{d}\varrho^{d}},\quad\text{so that}\quad\max_{|x|\leq r_{0}}\varphi(x)\leq\frac{I}{\omega_{d}(R-r_{0})^{d}}\,

for every
0<r_{0}<R
. Still using the fact that
\varphi
is nonnegative, (
3.18
)
with
r_{0}:=\frac{1}{2}R+\frac{1}{2}r
and
the estimate of

[
22
, Corollary 2.4]

yield the Lipschitz bound of (
3.16
).

The Legendre transform of
\psi
defined by

\psi^{*}(x):=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\psi(y)

coincides with
\varphi
in
{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)

(in particular it is finite in
{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)
) and
takes the value
+\infty
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

with
|x|>R
, since

\psi^{*}(x)\geq\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-R|y|=+\infty\quad\text{if $|x|>R$}.

Fenchel-Moreau Theorem
and (
3.7
)
then yield

\psi(y)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\psi^{*}(x)=\sup_{x\in\overline{{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\psi^{*}(x)=\sup_{x\in{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\psi^{*}(x)=\sup_{x\in{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\varphi(x)

thus showing (
3.17
);
in particular we get that
that
\psi
is
R
-Lipschitz.
∎

We conclude this part with the study of the stability properties of pairs of potentials.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
R,I>0
and let

\varphi_{n}:{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)\to\mathbb{R}
,
\psi_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, be two sequences of
(continuous and) convex functions satisfying
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

(3.19)

\begin{gathered}|\psi_{n}(y)|\leq R|y|\quad\text{for every }y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\varphi_{n}(x)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\psi_{n}(y)\ \text{for every }x\in{\mathrm{B}}(0,R),\quad\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}\varphi_{n}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq I.\end{gathered}

Then there exist a subsequence
j\mapsto n(j)
and two convex and
continuous functions
\varphi:{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)\to\mathbb{R}
and
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(i)

\varphi_{n(j)}\to\varphi
locally uniformly on
{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)
;

(ii)

\psi_{n(j)}\to\psi
locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
;

(iii)

\psi
is
R
-Lipschitz and

\nabla\psi_{n(j)}\to\nabla\psi

{\mathscr{L}}^{d}
-a.e. on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Moreover the pair
(\varphi,\psi)
satisfies (
3.15
),
(
3.16
), and (
3.17
).

Proof.

Thanks to (
3.19
) and Lemma
3.3
,
the sequence of pairs
(\varphi_{n},\psi_{n})
satisfies
the equicontinuity estimates (
3.16
) and (
3.17
)
with constants
R,I
independent of
n
.

By Arzelà-Ascoli Theorem, we can find a subsequence
j\mapsto n(j)
and
convex and continuous functions
\varphi:{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)\to\mathbb{R}
and

\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\varphi_{n(j)}\to\varphi
and

\psi_{n(j)}\to\psi
locally uniformly in their respective domains.

In particular,
\psi_{n(j)}
Mosco converges (see
e.g.
[
10
, Definition 3.17, Proposition 3.19]
)
to
\psi
and therefore the sequence of its Legendre transforms
\psi_{n(j)}^{*}
Mosco
converges to
\psi^{*}
(
[
10
, Theorem 3.18]
).
Since
\psi_{n}^{*}
coincides with
\varphi_{n}
in
{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)

and
\varphi_{n(j)}
converge locally uniformly to
\varphi
,
we deduce that
\varphi
coincides with
\psi^{*}
in
{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)
:

\varphi(x)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\psi(y)\quad\text{for every
}x\in{\mathrm{B}}(0,R).

By Fatou’s Lemma
\varphi
also satisfies the integral bound of
(
3.15
).
A further application of Lemma
3.3
yields (
3.16
)
and (
3.17
).

Finally, the local uniform convergence of
\psi_{n(j)}
to
\psi
gives
[
40
, Theorem 24.5]
the pointwise convergence of
\nabla\psi_{n(j)}(x)
to
\nabla\psi(x)
at every point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
where all the
\psi_{n(j)}
and
\psi
are differentiable. This proves (iii) and concludes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

4.
The Wasserstein Sobolev space

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})

In this section we consider the metric space
{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
,
endowed with the
L^{2}
-Wasserstein distance
{\sf d}=W_{2}
and a finite
positive Borel measure
\mathfrak{m}
.
We will denote by

\mathbb{W}_{2}=\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathfrak{m})
the metric-measure space
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})

and we want to study the Wasserstein Sobolev space
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
.

We will show that
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
is Hilbertian
(and therefore the metric space
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2})
is
infinitesimally Hilbertian) and its functions admit a nice
approximation in terms of the distinguished algebra of cylinder
functions.

4.1.
The algebra of
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
-cylinder functions

We denote by
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of bounded and Lipschitz
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}

functions
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
. This in particular implies that
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\phi(x)|+|\nabla\phi(x)|<+\infty
if
\phi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Every
\phi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
induces the
function
\mathsf{L}_{\phi}
on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(4.1)

\mathsf{L}_{\phi}:\mu\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu

which clearly belongs to
\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2})
thanks to
(
3.5
).
More generally, if
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}=(\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{N})\in\big({\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)^{N}
, we denote by

\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}:=(\mathsf{L}_{\phi_{1}},\cdots,\mathsf{L}_{\phi_{N}})
the corresponding map
from
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Our construction is based on the algebra of
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
- cylinder
functions generated by (
4.1
) via composition with
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
functions
and it is quite similar to the one of
[
17
, Section
2]
(see also
[
50
]
). Working in the flat space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
allows for a
further simplification in the structure of the tangent bundle and of
corresponding vector fields.

Definition 4.1

(
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
-Cylinder functions)
.

We say that a function
F:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is a
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
-
cylinder function
if there exist
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\psi\in{\mathrm{C}}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}=(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{N})\in({\mathrm{C}}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{N}
such that

(4.2)

F(\mu)=\psi(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(\mu))=\psi\big(\mathsf{L}_{\phi_{1}}(\mu),\cdots,\mathsf{L}_{\phi_{N}}(\mu)\big)\quad\text{for every }\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

We denote the set of such functions by
\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
.

Remark 4.2
.

Notice that
\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
is a unital subalgebra of

\mathop{\rm Lip}_{b}\nolimits(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2})
.
One could also consider the smaller algebra
\operatorname{FC}^{1}_{b}\!\!\left({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)

(resp.
\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)
)
generated by functions as in (
4.1
) (resp. by functions as in (
4.1
) where
\phi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
),
thus restricting
\psi
to be a polynomial in (
4.2
). This means that every element
F\in\operatorname{FC}^{1}_{b}\!\!\left({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)
(resp.
\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)
) can be written as

F=\psi\circ\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}

for some
\psi
polynomial in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\in({\mathrm{C}}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{N}
(resp.
({\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
) and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
N\geq 1
.
We prefer at this stage the choice of
\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
, since it
simplifies some technical points. However, Proposition

4.19
shows that
using

\operatorname{FC}^{1}_{b}\!\!\left({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)
or
\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)
will lead to the same
conclusions.

Remark 4.3
.

Since for every
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\in\big({\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)^{N}

the range of
\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}
is always contained in the
bounded set

[-M,M]^{N}
where
M:=\max_{i=1,\dots,d}\|\phi_{i}\|_{\infty}
,
also functions
F=\psi\circ\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}

with
\psi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
belong
to
\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
. Indeed it is enough to consider a function

\tilde{\psi}\in{\mathrm{C}}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
coinciding with
\psi
on

[-M,M]^{N}
and equal to
0
outside
[-M-1,M+1]^{N}

so that
F=\tilde{\psi}\circ\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}
.
In particular
every function of the form
\mathsf{L}_{\phi}
,
\phi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
belongs to
\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
.

Let us consider the set

(4.3)

\mathcal{D}:=\Big\{(\mu,x)\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:x\in\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mu)\Big\}.

The set
\mathcal{D}
is a Borel set (in fact it is a
G_{\delta}
): if
(r_{n})_{n}=\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,+\infty)
we have that
\mathcal{D}=\cap_{n}\mathcal{D}_{n}
, where

\mathcal{D}_{n}:=\left\{(\mu,x)\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mu({\mathrm{B}}(x,r_{n}))>0\right\},

and each
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is open in
{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, being the inverse image of
(0,+\infty)
through the lower semicontinuous map
(\mu,x)\mapsto\mu({\mathrm{B}}(x,r))
.

Definition 4.4
.

If
F=\psi\circ\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
as in
(
4.2
)
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\psi\in{\mathrm{C}}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\in({\mathrm{C}}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{N}
, then the Wasserstein differential of
F
,

{\mathrm{D}}F:\overline{\mathcal{D}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, is defined by

(4.4)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\partial_{n}\psi\left(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(\mu)\right)\nabla\phi_{n}(x),\quad(\mu,x)\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}.

We will also denote by
{\mathrm{D}}F[\mu]
the
function
x\mapsto{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x)
and we will set

(4.5)

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathrm{D}}F\left[\mu\right]\right\|_{\mu}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|{\mathrm{D}}F[\mu](x)|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x),\quad\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Remark 4.5
.

It is not difficult to check that

(4.6)

{\mathrm{D}}F\text{ is continuous in }\overline{\mathcal{D}}

with respect to the natural product (narrow and euclidean) topology
of
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

In principle

{\mathrm{D}}F
(and thus
\left\|{\mathrm{D}}F\left[\mu\right]\right\|_{\mu}
) may depend on the choice of
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\psi\in{\mathrm{C}}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\in({\mathrm{C}}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{N}

used to represent
F
. In Proposition
4.9
we show
that for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

the function
{\mathrm{D}}F[\mu]
is uniquely characterized in
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mu)

and
\left\|{\mathrm{D}}F\left[\mu\right]\right\|_{\mu}
is well defined, so that
{\mathrm{D}}F
is
uniquely characterized by
F
in
\mathcal{D}
. By (
4.6
),
{\mathrm{D}}F
is
also uniquely
characterized by
F
on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
.

We have seen that the Wasserstein differential
{\mathrm{D}}F
can
be considered as a map from
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
with values in

\mathbb{R}^{d}
. It is natural to introduce the
measure
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}=\int\delta_{\mu}\otimes\mu\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
obtained integrating
the measures
\mu
w.r.t.
\mathfrak{m}
: for every bounded Borel function

H:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
we have

(4.7)

\int H(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x)=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}H(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu).

Since
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}}
,
it is then clear that
{\mathrm{D}}F
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})

and

(4.8)

\|{\mathrm{D}}F\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left\|{\mathrm{D}}F\left[\mu\right]\right\|_{\mu}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)=\int_{\overline{\mathcal{D}}}|{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x).

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
Y
be a Polish space
and let
G:\mathcal{P}(Y)\times Y\to[0,+\infty)
be a bounded and
continuous function. If

(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence in
\mathcal{P}(Y)
narrowly converging
to
\mu
as
n\to+\infty
, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Y}G(\mu_{n},y){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{n}(y)=\int_{Y}G(\mu,y){\mathrm{d}}\mu(y).

Proof.

We set
g_{n}(x):=G(\mu_{n},x),\ g(x):=G(\mu,x)
.
Since
G
is continuous,
g_{n}
converge uniformly to
g
on compact
subsets of
Y
as
n\to\infty
. Thanks to

[
4
, Lemma 5.2.1]

(g_{n})_{\sharp}\mu_{n}
converge narrowly to

g_{\sharp}\mu
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
. On the other hand, the support of

(g_{n})_{\sharp}\mu_{n}
is uniformly bounded because
G
is bounded so that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Y}G(\mu_{n},y){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{n}(y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r\,{\mathrm{d}}((g_{n})_{\sharp}\mu_{n})(r)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}r\,{\mathrm{d}}(g_{\sharp}\mu)(r)=\int_{Y}G(\mu,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y).\qed

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
F=\psi\circ\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)

as in (
4.2
)
and let
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be an absolutely continuous curve in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

(4.9)

F(\mu_{1})-F(\mu_{0})=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}{\mathrm{D}}F[\mu_{t}](x),v_{t}(x){\rangle}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

where
v_{t}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu_{t};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the Wasserstein velocity field (cf. Theorem
3.1
) of
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
at time
t
and
{\mathrm{D}}F
is as in (
4.4
).

In case the curve
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
admits the parametrization

\mu_{t}:=\big(\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\big)_{\sharp}\mu,\quad t\in[0,1],

for some Borel probability measure
\mu
in a Polish space
\Omega
and some map
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}([0,1];L^{2}(\Omega,\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
then
F\circ\mu\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}([0,1])
and

(4.10)

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}F(\mu_{t})=\int_{\Omega}{\langle}{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu_{t},\boldsymbol{x}_{t}(\omega)),\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}_{t}(\omega)){\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(\omega)\quad\text{for every }t\in[0,1].

Proof.

Observe that, since
F
is Lipschitz continuous and
t\mapsto\mu_{t}
is absolutely continuous, the map
t\mapsto F(\mu_{t})
is absolutely continuous and thus it holds

F(\mu_{1})-F(\mu_{0})=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}F(\mu_{t}){\mathrm{d}}t.

It is then enough to prove that

(4.11)

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}F(\mu_{t})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu_{t},x),v_{t}(x){\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\quad\text{for a.e. }t\in(0,1).

We have, for every
t\in A((\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]})\subset(0,1)
(cf. Theorem
3.1
), that

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}F(\mu_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{i}\psi(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(\mu_{t}))\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{i}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{i}\psi(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(\mu_{t}))\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}\nabla\phi_{i},v_{t}(x){\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu_{t},x),v_{t}(x){\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x),

where we used Theorem
3.1
.
A completely analogous argument
yields (
4.10
).
∎

Remark 4.8
.

Consider the case in which the curve
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
has the simple form

\mu_{t}:=({\mbox{\boldmath$i$}}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+tu)_{\sharp}\mu,\quad t\in[0,1]

for some map
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
{\mbox{\boldmath$i$}}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
denotes the identity map on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then
(
4.10
)
yields

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}F(\mu_{t})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu_{t},x),u(x){\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\quad\text{for every }t\in[0,1],

and, in particular, we get

(4.12)

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{F(\mu_{t})-F(\mu)}{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x),u(x)){\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x).

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
F=\psi\circ\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)

as in (
4.2
). Then

\left\|{\mathrm{D}}F\left[\mu\right]\right\|_{\mu}=\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits F(\mu)\quad\text{ for every }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

In particular
\left\|{\mathrm{D}}F\left[\mu\right]\right\|_{\mu}
does not depend on the choice of the
representation of
F

and
{\mathrm{D}}F
just depends on
F
on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
.

Proof.

Let
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and let
(\mu^{\prime}_{n},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n})\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}
with
\mu^{\prime}_{n}\neq\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n}
be such that
(\mu^{\prime}_{n},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n})\to(\mu,\mu)
in
W_{2}
and

\lim_{n}\frac{\left|F(\mu^{\prime}_{n})-F(\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n})\right|}{W_{2}(\mu^{\prime}_{n},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n})}=\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits F(\mu).

Let us define, for every
t\in[0,1]
, the map
{\sf x}^{t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
as

{\sf x}^{t}(x_{0},x_{1}):=(1-t)x_{0}+tx_{1},\quad(x_{0},x_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Using (
4.10
)
along
\mu_{n}^{t}:={\sf x}^{t}_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{n}

for plans
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{n}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu^{\prime}_{n},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n})

(it is easy to check that
(\mu_{n}^{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is Lipschitz
continuous), we get

\displaystyle\left|F(\mu^{\prime}_{n})-F(\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n})\right|=\left|\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu_{n}^{t},{\sf x}^{t}(x_{0},x_{1})),x_{1}-x_{0}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{n}(x_{0},x_{1})\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu_{n}^{t},{\sf x}^{t}(x_{0},x_{1})))\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{n}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x_{1}-x_{0}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{n}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=W_{2}(\mu^{\prime}_{n},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n})\left(\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu_{n}^{t},x)\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{t}_{n}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where we used Theorem
3.1
. Dividing both sides by
W_{2}(\mu^{\prime}_{n},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n})
, we obtain

\frac{\left|F(\mu^{\prime}_{n})-F(\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n})\right|}{W_{2}(\mu^{\prime}_{n},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{n})}\leq\left(\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu_{n}^{t},x)\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{t}_{n}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Observe that
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}_{n}\to{\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}}:=({\mbox{\boldmath$i$}}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}},{\mbox{\boldmath$i$}}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}})_{\sharp}\mu
narrowly in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\to+\infty
so that
\mu_{n}^{t}\to\mu
narrowly in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\to+\infty
for every
t\in[0,1]
.
We can pass to the limit as
n\to+\infty
the above inequality using the dominated convergence Theorem and Lemma
4.6
with

G(\mu,x):=\left|\sum_{n=1}^{N}\partial_{n}\psi\left(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(\mu)\right)\nabla\phi_{n}(x)\right|^{2},\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

which provides a continuous and bounded extension (depending on the particular choice of
\psi
and
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}
) of
|{\mathrm{D}}F|^{2}
to
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We hence get

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits F(\mu)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x)\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\left\|{\mathrm{D}}F\left[\mu\right]\right\|_{\mu}.

This proves one inequality. In order to prove the opposite one, it is not restrictive to assume

\left\|{\mathrm{D}}F\left[\mu\right]\right\|_{\mu}>0
. Let us now consider the map
T:\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mu)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined as

T(x):={\mathrm{D}}F[\mu](x),\quad x\in\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mu).

By definition of
\Tan_{\mu}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we have that
T\in\Tan_{\mu}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
so that, by
[
4
, Proposition 8.5.6]
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{W_{2}(\mu,({\mbox{\boldmath$i$}}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+\varepsilon T)_{\sharp}\mu)}{\varepsilon}=\|T\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\left\|{\mathrm{D}}F\left[\mu\right]\right\|_{\mu}.

Moreover, if we apply (
4.12
) to the curve
\mu_{\varepsilon}:=({\mbox{\boldmath$i$}}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+\varepsilon T)_{\sharp}\mu
,
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
, we get

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{F(\mu_{\varepsilon})-F(\mu)}{\varepsilon}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x),T(x)){\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=\left\|{\mathrm{D}}F\left[\mu\right]\right\|_{\mu}^{2},

thus

\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits F(\mu)\geq\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{F(\mu_{\varepsilon})-F(\mu)}{W_{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\mu)}=\left\|{\mathrm{D}}F\left[\mu\right]\right\|_{\mu}.

This shows the other inequality and concludes the proof.
∎

4.2.
The density result

Recall that for a bounded Lipschitz function

F:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
the pre-Cheeger energy (cf. (
2.15
)) associated to
\mathfrak{m}
is defined by

(4.13)

\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F)=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits F(\mu)\big)^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu).

Thanks to Proposition
4.9
, if
F
is a cylinder
function in
\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
, we have a nice equivalent expression

(4.14)

\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F)=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left\|{\mathrm{D}}F\left[\mu\right]\right\|_{\mu}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)=\int|{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x),

which shows that the restriction of
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
to
\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
is a
quadratic form (thus satisfying (
2.59
)) induced by the bilinear
form

(4.15)

\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F,G):=\int{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x)\cdot{\mathrm{D}}G(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x),\quad F,G\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)

and coincides with the typical bilinear forms on cylinder
functions used in

[
50
,
47
,
17
,
18
]
.
It is therefore important to prove that
\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
is dense in energy
and therefore
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
is a Hilbert space:
this is precisely the object of our main result.

Theorem 4.10
.

The algebra
\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
is
dense in
2
-energy:
for every
F\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})

there exists a sequence
F_{n}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
,
n\in\mathbb{N},
such that

(4.16)

F_{n}\to F\ \text{ $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.,}\quad\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits(F_{n})\to|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star}\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m});

if moreover
F\in L^{p}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
,
p\in[1,+\infty)
, then
we can find a sequence
F_{n}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
as in (
4.16
)
and converging to
F
in
L^{p}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
.

Corollary 4.11
.

H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
is a separable Hilbert space and
\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
is
strongly dense in
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
.
If
\big(\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2},\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)\big)
is closable (recall Remark

2.19
)
then
its smallest closed extension coincides with
(\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2},H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2}))
.

According to the terminology introduced in
[
24
]
(see
also
[
7
]
)
we can say that
({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},W_{2},\mathfrak{m})
is infinitesimally Hilbertian for
every positive Borel measure
\mathfrak{m}
.

We devote the remaining part of this subsection to the proof this
result, using Theorem
2.13
.

We adopt the notation
\mathscr{A}:=\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
.

We start with a preliminary lemma, which provides a simple
gradient estimate for the distance from
the Dirac mass centered at 0, i.e. the quadratic moment of a measure.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\vartheta\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a
L
-Lipschitz function which is
continuously differentiable in the open set
\Omega_{\vartheta}:=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\vartheta(x)\neq 0\big\}
.
Then the
map

(4.17)

F:\mu\to\big(\mathsf{L}_{\vartheta^{2}}(\mu)\big)^{1/2}=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\vartheta^{2}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)^{1/2}

is
L
-Lipschitz and belongs to
D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathscr{A})
, in particular
its
(2,\mathscr{A})
-relaxed gradient is bounded above by
L
and satisfies

(4.18)

|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}^{2}(\mu)\leq\frac{1}{F^{2}(\mu)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\vartheta^{2}|\nabla\vartheta|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ with $F(\mu)>0$}.

Proof.

Let
T\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be an odd, nondecreasing truncation function
satisfying

(4.19)

T(x)=x\quad\text{if }|x|\leq 1/2,\quad|T(x)|=1\quad\text{if }|x|\geq 2,\quad|T^{\prime}(x)|\leq 1,

and let us set
T_{n}(x):=nT(x/n)
,
\vartheta_{n}:=T_{n}\circ\vartheta
, so that

\vartheta_{n}
is
L
-Lipschitz and continuously differentiable in

\Omega_{\vartheta}
, so that

\vartheta_{n}^{2}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

We define
\psi_{n}(r):=(r+1/n)^{1/2}
and

F_{n}:=\psi_{n}\circ\mathsf{L}_{\vartheta_{n}^{2}}
.
By construction
F_{n}\in\mathscr{A}
with

\displaystyle{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}(\mu,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{F_{n}(\mu)}\vartheta_{n}(x)\nabla\vartheta_{n}(x),

(4.20)

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits F_{n}(\mu)\big)^{2}=\|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}[\mu]\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{F^{2}_{n}(\mu)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\vartheta_{n}^{2}(x)|\nabla\vartheta_{n}(x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\leq L^{2}.

Since
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2})
is a length space we deduce that
F_{n}

is
L
-Lipschitz. On the other hand
\lim_{n\to\infty}F_{n}(\mu)=F(\mu)
pointwise
everywhere, so that
F
is
L
-Lipschitz as well, it belongs to

D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathscr{A})
and
|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq L
.
Passing eventually to the limit as
n\to\infty
in (
4.20
)
for
\mu
in the open set
\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):F(\mu)>0\}
we
get (
4.18
).
∎

Selecting
\vartheta(x):=|x|
and applying the first part of Lemma
4.12
we immediately get the following corollary.

Corollary 4.13
.

The function
\mathsf{m}_{2}(\cdot)
as in (
3.6
) belongs to

D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathscr{A})
with

(4.21)

|{\mathrm{D}}\mathsf{m}_{2}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(\mu)\leq 1\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

We now use
{\sf m}_{2}
for localizing gradient estimates in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
F_{n}
be a sequence of functions in
D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathscr{A})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
such
that
F_{n}
and

|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}
are uniformly bounded in every bounded set
of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and let
F,G
be Borel functions in

L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
,
G
nonnegative.
If

(4.22)

\lim_{n\to\infty}F_{n}(\mu)=F(\mu),\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(\mu)\leq G(\mu)\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in $\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$},

then
F\in H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathscr{A})
and
|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq G
.

Proof.

Let us consider a smooth nonincreasing function
\theta\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}[0,+\infty)
such
that

(4.23)

\theta(r)=1\quad\text{if }0\leq r\leq 1,\quad\theta(r)=0\quad\text{if }r\geq 2,\quad|\theta^{\prime}(r)|\leq 2

and set

(4.24)

{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{n}(\mu):=\theta\big({\sf m}_{2}(\mu)/n\big).

By Corollary
4.13
we have

(4.25)

{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{n}\in H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathscr{A}),\quad|{\mathrm{D}}{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{n}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq 2/n,\quad|{\mathrm{D}}{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{n}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(\mu)=0\text{ if }\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu)\leq n\text{ or }\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu)\geq 2n.

Thanks to the Leibniz rule, setting
F_{n,m}(\mu):=F_{n}(\mu){\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}^{2}_{m}(\mu)
and
G_{n}:=|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}
, we have

(4.26)

F_{n,m}\in{\mathrm{D}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathscr{A}),\quad|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n,m}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(\mu)\leq G_{n}(\mu){\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}^{2}_{m}(\mu)+4/mF_{n}(\mu){\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{m}(\mu).

Since for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
the sequence

n\mapsto G_{n}{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{m}^{2}
is uniformly bounded, we can find an
increasing subsequence
k\mapsto n(k)
such that

k\mapsto G_{n(k)}{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{m}^{2}
is
weakly
∗
convergent in
L^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
and we
denote by
\tilde{G}_{m}
is
weak
∗
limit. By Fatou’s lemma, for every Borel set

B\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we get

\displaystyle\int_{B}\tilde{G}_{m}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{B}G_{n(k)}(\mu){\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}^{2}_{m}(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B}\limsup_{k\to\infty}\Big(G_{n(k)}(\mu){\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}^{2}_{m}(\mu)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B}G^{2}{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{m}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}

so that we deduce

(4.27)

\tilde{G}_{m}\leq G^{2}{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{m}^{2}\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in
$\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, for every $m\in\mathbb{N}$.}

On the other hand, passing to the limit in (
4.26
) along the
subsequence
n(k)
and recalling that

\lim_{k\to\infty}F_{n(k),m}=F{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{m}^{2}

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. we get

(4.28)

|{\mathrm{D}}(F{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{m}^{2})|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(\mu)\leq\tilde{G}_{m}(\mu)+\frac{4}{m}F(\mu){\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{m}(\mu)\leq G(\mu){\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{m}^{2}(\mu)+\frac{4}{m}F(\mu){\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{m}(\mu)\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$.}

We eventually pass to the limit as
m\to\infty
concluding the proof
of the Lemma.
∎

We now derive a natural estimate, extending (
4.4
) to the
case of quadratically coercive functions whose gradient has a linear growth.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\phi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be satisfying the growth conditions

(4.29)

\phi(x)\geq A|x|^{2}-B,\quad|\nabla\phi(x)|\leq C(|x|+1)\quad\text{for
every }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

for given positive constants
A,B,C>0

and let
\zeta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
nondecreasing
function whose derivative has compact support.
Then
the function

F(\mu):=\zeta\circ\mathsf{L}_{\phi}

is Lipschitz in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
it
belongs to

H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathscr{A})
, and

(4.30)

|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(\mu)\leq\zeta^{\prime}(\mathsf{L}_{\phi}(\mu))\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla\phi(x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)^{1/2}.

Proof.

We set
\zeta_{a}(z):=(z+a)^{1/2}
and
\vartheta_{a}:=\zeta_{a}\circ\phi
, with
a:=A+B
, so that

\vartheta_{a}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\vartheta_{a}\geq\big(A(|x|^{2}+1)\big)^{1/2},\quad|\nabla\vartheta_{a}(x)|=\frac{|\nabla\phi(x)|}{2(\phi(x)+a)^{1/2}}\leq L,\quad L:=A^{-1/2}C

for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We can then apply Lemma
4.12
, observing that

\big(\mathsf{L}_{\vartheta_{a}^{2}}(\mu)\big)^{1/2}=\zeta_{a}\big(\mathsf{L}_{\phi}(\mu)\big);

we deduce that
F_{a}=\zeta_{a}\circ\mathsf{L}_{\phi}
is
L
-Lipschitz, it belongs to

D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathscr{A})
and satisfies (recall (
4.18
))

(4.31)

|{\mathrm{D}}F_{a}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(\mu)\leq\frac{1}{2F_{a}(\mu)}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla(\vartheta_{a}^{2})|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\Big)^{1/2}=\frac{1}{2F_{a}(\mu)}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla\phi|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\Big)^{1/2}.

We eventually observe that

F=\psi_{a}\circ F_{a}
, where
\psi_{a}(z)=\zeta(z^{2}-a)
is a
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
Lipschitz function since
\zeta^{\prime}
has compact support.
By the chain rule in Theorem
2.3
(7) we get that

|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}=|\psi^{\prime}_{a}\circ F_{a}||{\mathrm{D}}F_{a}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}=2F_{a}\zeta^{\prime}(\mathsf{L}_{\phi})|{\mathrm{D}}F_{a}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}.

Then (
4.31
) yields (
4.30
).
∎

We collect in the following definition
some useful tools and notation we will
extensively use.

Definition 4.16
.

We denote by

\kappa\in{\mathrm{C}}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

a smooth function satisfying
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\kappa}=\overline{{\mathrm{B}}(0,1)}
,
\kappa(x)\geq 0
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\kappa(x)>0
for every
x\in{\mathrm{B}}(0,1)
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\kappa{\mathrm{d}}{\mathscr{L}}^{d}=1
and
\kappa(-x)=\kappa(x)
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

For every
0<\varepsilon<1
we define the family of associated mollifiers

(4.32)

\kappa_{\varepsilon}(x):=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{d}}\kappa(x/\varepsilon)\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

and for every
\sigma\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
we define

(4.33)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\sigma\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon},

(4.34)

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)+\varepsilon^{d+3}{\mathscr{L}}^{d}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)}{\sigma_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon))+\varepsilon^{d+3}{\mathscr{L}}^{d}({\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon))}.

For every
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
we eventually define the continuous functions
W_{\nu},W_{\nu}^{\varepsilon},F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}:{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to\mathbb{R}
as

(4.35)

W_{\nu}(\mu):=W_{2}(\mu,\nu),\quad W_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu):=W_{\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon}}(\mu_{\varepsilon}),\quad F^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mu):=\frac{1}{2}(W_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu))^{2},\quad\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Notice that
\sigma_{\varepsilon},\hat{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\hat{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}}=\overline{{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)}
and
W_{2}(\sigma_{\varepsilon},\sigma)\to 0
,
W_{2}(\hat{\sigma}_{\varepsilon},\sigma)\to 0
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. Moreover, if
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, we have

(4.36)

W_{2}(\sigma_{\varepsilon},\sigma^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})\leq W_{2}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\quad\text{ for every }0<\varepsilon<1

and it is easy to check that,
if we set

(4.37)

C_{\varepsilon}:=\mathsf{m}_{2}(\kappa_{\varepsilon}{\mathscr{L}}^{d}),

then we have

(4.38)

\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon})\leq\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu)+C_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{ for every }0<\varepsilon<1.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and let
\zeta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
nondecreasing function whose derivative has compact support. With the notation of Definition

4.16

we have

(4.39)

|{\mathrm{D}}(\zeta\circ F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon})|_{\star,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{A}}}(\mu)\leq\zeta^{\prime}(F^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mu))\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)^{1/2}\quad\text{ for }\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e. }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

where
\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}=\Phi^{*}(\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\mu_{\varepsilon})
as in Theorem
3.2
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}:=\{\mu^{h}\}_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
be a dense and countable set
in
{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and let us set, for every
h\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}:=\Phi(\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\mu^{h}_{\varepsilon})
,
\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}^{*}:=\Phi^{*}(\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\mu^{h}_{\varepsilon})
(see Theorem
3.2
),

a_{\varepsilon,h}:=\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)}\left(\frac{1}{2}|y|^{2}-\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}(y)\right){\mathrm{d}}\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(y),\quad u_{\varepsilon,h}(x):=\frac{1}{2}|x|^{2}-\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}^{*}(x)+a_{\varepsilon,h},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and

G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu):=\max_{1\leq h\leq k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\varepsilon,h}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon},\quad\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

We first observe that
\varphi^{*}_{\varepsilon,h}(x)
is
1/\varepsilon
-Lipschitz (cf. Theorem
3.2
), so that

|\varphi^{*}_{\varepsilon,h}(x)|\leq|x|/\varepsilon
and

(4.40)

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon,h}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}|x|^{2}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}|x|+a_{\varepsilon,h}\geq\frac{1}{4}|x|^{2}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}+a_{\varepsilon,h},

(4.41)

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon,h}(x)

\displaystyle\leq|x|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}+a_{\varepsilon,h}.

Claim 1
. It holds

\lim_{k\to+\infty}G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)=F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu)\quad\text{ for every }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Proof of claim 1
. Since
G_{\varepsilon,k+1}(\mu)\geq G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)
for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, we have that

\lim_{k\to+\infty}G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)=\sup_{k}G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)=\sup_{h}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\varepsilon,h}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{ for every }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

By the definition of
\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}
and
\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}^{*}
(see (
3.9
)) we have that

\frac{1}{2}|x|^{2}-\varphi^{*}_{\varepsilon,h}(x)+\frac{1}{2}|y|^{2}-\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}(y)\leq\frac{1}{2}|x-y|^{2}\quad\text{ for every }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,y\in{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon),

so that for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
h\in\mathbb{N}
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\varepsilon,h}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\frac{1}{2}|x|^{2}-\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}^{*}(x)\right){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}+\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)}\left(\frac{1}{2}|y|^{2}-\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}(y)\right){\mathrm{d}}\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu),

where
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon})
.
This proves that
\sup_{k}G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)\leq F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu)
for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. If
\mu\in\mathcal{G}
, then we can find
h\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mu=\mu^{h}
so that, by definition of
\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}
and
\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}^{*}
, we obtain that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\varepsilon,h}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\frac{1}{2}|x|^{2}-\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}^{*}(x)\right){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}+\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)}\left(\frac{1}{2}|y|^{2}-\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}(y)\right){\mathrm{d}}\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(y)=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon}^{h},\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon}).

Hence, if
\mu\in\mathcal{G}
, then
\sup_{k}G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)=F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu)
.

Let now
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
h\in\mathbb{N}
and observe that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\varepsilon,h}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\varepsilon,h}{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon})-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}^{*}{\mathrm{d}}(\mu_{\varepsilon}-\mu^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\Big(\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon})+\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})\Big)W_{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\mu^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}W_{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\mu^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\Big(\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu)+\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu^{\prime})+2C_{\varepsilon}\Big)W_{2}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}W_{2}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu)+\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu^{\prime})+C_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Big)W_{2}(\mu,\mu^{\prime}),

where we used (
4.36
), (
4.38
), the fact that
\varphi^{*}_{\varepsilon,h}
is
1/\varepsilon
-Lipschitz continuous and (
3.5
). We hence deduce that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}

(4.42)

\left|G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)-G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu^{\prime})\right|\leq\left(\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu)+\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu^{\prime})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+C_{\varepsilon}\right)W_{2}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\quad\text{ for every }\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Choosing
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
and passing to the limit as
k\to+\infty
we get from (
4.42
)

\left|\lim_{k\to+\infty}G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)-F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu^{\prime})\right|\leq\left(\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu)+\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu^{\prime})+C_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)W_{2}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\quad\text{ for every }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}.

Using the density of
\mathcal{G}
and the continuity of
\mu^{\prime}\mapsto F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu^{\prime})
we deduce that

\lim_{k\to+\infty}G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)=F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu)\quad\text{ for every }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

proving the first claim.

Claim 2
.
If
H_{\varepsilon,k}:=\zeta\circ G_{\varepsilon,k}
and
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon,h}:=u_{\varepsilon,h}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
it holds

|{\mathrm{D}}H_{\varepsilon,k}|_{\star,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{A}}}^{2}(\mu)\leq\big(\zeta^{\prime}(G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu))\big)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\nabla\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon,h}\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=\big(\zeta^{\prime}(G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu))\big)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}^{*}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x),

for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
\mu\in B_{\varepsilon,h}^{k}
, where
B_{\varepsilon,h}^{k}:=\{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mid G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\varepsilon,h}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}\}
,
h\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

Proof of claim 2
. For every
h\in\mathbb{N}
,
(
4.40
) yields

(4.43)

\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon,h}(x)\geq\frac{1}{4}|x|^{2}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}+a_{\varepsilon,h},\quad|\nabla\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon,h}(x)|\leq|x|+\frac{1}{\varepsilon};

where we used that

|x|^{2}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon}\geq|x\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon}|^{2}=|x|^{2},\quad\nabla u_{\varepsilon,h}(x)=x-\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}^{*}(x),\quad\nabla u_{\varepsilon,h}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon}=x-\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon,h}^{*}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon}.

Since the map
\ell_{\varepsilon,h}:{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to\mathbb{R}
defined as
\ell_{\varepsilon,h}(\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\varepsilon,h}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}
satisfies

\ell_{\varepsilon,h}(\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u_{\varepsilon,h}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon}){\mathrm{d}}\mu=\mathsf{L}_{\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon,h}}(\mu),\quad\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

Lemma
4.15
and the
above estimates yield

|{\mathrm{D}}(\zeta\circ\ell_{\varepsilon,h})|_{\star}(\mu)\leq\zeta^{\prime}(\ell_{\varepsilon,h}(\mu))\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\nabla\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon,h}\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu\Big)^{1/2}\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e. }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Since
\zeta
is nondecreasing,
H_{\varepsilon,k}
can be written as

H_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)=\max_{1\leq h\leq k}(\zeta\circ\ell_{\varepsilon,h})(\mu),\quad\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

so that we can apply Theorem
2.3
(8) and conclude the proof of
the second claim.

Claim 3
. Let
(h_{n})_{n}\subset\mathbb{N}
be a non-decreasing
sequence and
let
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. If
\lim_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\varepsilon,h_{n}}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}=F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu)
, then

\lim_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\varphi_{\varepsilon,h_{n}}^{*}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x),

where
\varphi^{*}_{\varepsilon}=\Phi^{*}(\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\mu_{\varepsilon})
.

Proof of claim 3
.
Let us set for every
n\in\mathbb{N}

\phi_{\varepsilon,n}:=\varphi_{\varepsilon,h_{n}},\quad\psi_{\varepsilon,n}=\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon,n}:=\varphi^{*}_{\varepsilon,h_{n}}.

We will show that from any (non relabeled) increasing subsequence it is
possible to extract a further subsequence
j\mapsto{n(j)}
such
that

\lim_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon,{n(j)}}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x).

By Theorem
3.2
, we have that, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon,n}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is convex and
1/\varepsilon
-Lipschitz continuous
with
\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon,n}(0)=0
,
\phi_{\varepsilon,n}:{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)\to\mathbb{R}
is convex and continuous and

\phi_{\varepsilon,n}(x)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}-\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon,n}(y)\quad\text{ for every }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Moreover, since
F^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}\geq 0
and
|\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon,n}(x)|\leq\varepsilon^{-1}|x|
,
for
n
sufficiently large we have

(4.44)

a_{\varepsilon,h_{n}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(u_{\varepsilon,h_{n}}(x)+\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon,n}(x)-\frac{1}{2}|x|^{2}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}(x)\geq-C(\varepsilon,\mu)

with
C(\varepsilon,\mu):=1+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}{\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon})}
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)}\phi_{\varepsilon,n}\,{\mathrm{d}}\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}(\hat{\nu}^{\varepsilon})-a_{\varepsilon,h_{n}}\leq C^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\mu,\nu)

where
C^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\mu,\nu):=C(\varepsilon,\mu)+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon})
.
Since
\hat{\nu}^{\varepsilon}\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{d+3}}{1+\varepsilon^{3}\omega_{d}}{\mathscr{L}}^{d}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)

we can find a constant
I=I(\varepsilon,\mu,\nu)
such that

\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)}\phi_{\varepsilon,n}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq I
for sufficiently large
n
.

Thus by Lemma
3.4
, we get the existence of a subsequence

j\mapsto n(j)
and two convex continuous functions
\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\phi_{\varepsilon}:{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)\to\mathbb{R}

such that points (i), (ii), (iii) and conclusions of Lemma
3.4
hold.
By points (i) and (ii) we can use Fatou Lemma and the dominated convergence Theorem to conclude that

\liminf_{j}\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)}\phi_{\varepsilon,n(j)}{\mathrm{d}}\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\geq\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)}\phi_{\varepsilon}{\mathrm{d}}\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\quad\lim_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon,n(j)}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}.

We thus deduce that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\frac{1}{2}|x|^{2}-\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon}(x)\right){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}(x)+\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)}\left(\frac{1}{2}|y|^{2}-\phi_{\varepsilon}(y)\right){\mathrm{d}}\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(y)\geq\limsup_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\varepsilon,h_{n(j)}}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}=F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu)

proving that

\int_{{\mathrm{B}}(0,1/\varepsilon)}\phi_{\varepsilon}\,{\mathrm{d}}\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon})+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{m}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon})-\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\mu_{\varepsilon}).

By the uniqueness part of Theorem
3.2

we deduce that
\phi_{\varepsilon}=\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\Phi(\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\mu_{\varepsilon})
and
\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon}=\varphi^{*}_{\varepsilon}=\Phi^{*}(\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\mu_{\varepsilon})
. Finally, the a.e. convergence of
the gradient of
\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon,n}
to the gradient of
\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon}
given by point
(iii) in Lemma
3.4
gives that
\nabla(\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon,n(j)}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})\to\nabla(\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})
pointwise
everywhere. Moreover, since for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have
x\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon}=x
and

\left|x-\nabla(\varphi^{*}_{\varepsilon,n(j)}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}\leq\big(|x|+1/\varepsilon\big)^{2}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu),

we can use the dominated convergence Theorem to conclude that

\lim_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon,n(j)}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\phi^{*}_{\varepsilon}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x).

This concludes the proof of the third claim.

Claim 4
. It holds

\limsup_{k}|{\mathrm{D}}H_{\varepsilon,k}|_{\star,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{A}}}(\mu)\leq\zeta^{\prime}(F^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mu))\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)^{1/2}\quad\text{ for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e. }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

where
\varphi^{*}_{\varepsilon}=\Phi^{*}(\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\mu_{\varepsilon})
.

Proof of claim 4
. Let
B_{\varepsilon}\subset{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
be defined as

B_{\varepsilon}:=\bigcap_{k}\bigcup_{h=1}^{k}A^{k}_{\varepsilon,h},

where
A^{k}_{\varepsilon,h}
is the full
\mathfrak{m}
-measure subset of
B_{\varepsilon,h}^{k}
where claim 2 holds. Notice that
B_{\varepsilon}
has full
\mathfrak{m}
-measure. Let
\mu\in B_{\varepsilon}
be fixed and let us pick a non-decreasing sequence
k\mapsto h_{k}
such that

G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\varepsilon,h_{k}}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}.

By claim 1 we know that
G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)\to F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu)
so that we can apply claim 4 and conclude that

(4.45)

\zeta^{\prime}(F^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mu))\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=\lim_{k}\zeta^{\prime}(G_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu))\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\varphi^{*}_{\varepsilon,h_{k}}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x).

By claim 2, the right hand side is greater than
\limsup_{k}|{\mathrm{D}}H_{\varepsilon,k}|_{\star,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{A}}}^{2}(\mu)
; this concludes the proof of the fourth claim.

Conclusion.

We conclude the proof applying Lemma
4.14
with
(H_{\varepsilon,k})_{k}
in the role of
(F_{n})_{n}
,
F:=\zeta\circ F_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}
and
G
given by

G(\mu):=\zeta^{\prime}(F^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mu))\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)^{1/2},\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

We check that the hypotheses of Lemma
4.14
are satisfied: by Claim 2 we have that
H_{\varepsilon,k}\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathscr{A})
and it is also in
L^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
since
\zeta
is uniformly bounded.
Notice that, by (
4.43
), for every
R>0
it holds that

(4.46)

\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\nabla\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon,h}(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)^{1/2}\leq R+1/\varepsilon\quad\text{whenever }\mathsf{m}_{2}(\mu)\leq R.

This gives, also using Claim 2, that
|{\mathrm{D}}H_{\varepsilon,k}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}
is uniformly bounded on bounded subsets of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(recall that
\zeta^{\prime}
is uniformly bounded). It is also clear that
H_{\varepsilon,k}
is uniformly bounded on bounded subsets of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
since it is uniformly bounded by the infinity norm of
\zeta
.

The function
F
, being bounded again by the infinity norm of
\zeta
, belongs to
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
. The same holds for
G
: using (
4.45
) and passing to the limit the estimate in (
4.46
) we see that
G
is uniformly bounded, having
\zeta^{\prime}
compact support.

By Claim 1 and Claim 4 we have

\lim_{k\to+\infty}H_{\varepsilon,k}(\mu)=F(\mu),\quad\limsup_{k\to+\infty}|{\mathrm{D}}H_{\varepsilon,k}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(\mu)\leq G(\mu)\quad\text{ for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

By Lemma
4.14
we get (
4.39
).
∎

We still keep the notation of
Definition
4.16
.

Corollary 4.18
.

Let
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. Then

(4.47)

|{\mathrm{D}}W_{\nu}|_{\star,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{A}}}(\mu)\leq 1\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e. }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Proof.

First of all we prove that for every
0<\varepsilon<1
, it holds

(4.48)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\leq W^{2}_{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon})\quad\text{ for every }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

where
\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}=\Phi^{*}(\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\mu_{\varepsilon})
as in Theorem
3.2
.
Since

\left|x-\nabla(\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}\leq\left|x-\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}(x)\right|^{2}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon}(x)\quad\text{ for every }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

we get that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla(\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\left|x-\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}(x)\right|^{2}\ast\kappa_{\varepsilon}(x)\right){\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|x-\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{*}(x)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=W^{2}_{2}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\hat{\nu}_{\varepsilon}),

for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, where the last equality comes from
Theorem
3.2
. This proves (
4.48
).
It follows from Proposition
4.17
that, for every
nondecreasing function
\zeta\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
whose derivative has compact support, it holds

(4.49)

|{\mathrm{D}}\,(\zeta\circ F^{\varepsilon}_{\nu})|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(\mu)\leq\zeta^{\prime}(F^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mu))\sqrt{2F^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mu)}\quad\text{ for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$}.

Let us now consider a sequence of continuous and compactly supported functions
\alpha_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

0\leq\alpha_{n}(s)\uparrow\frac{{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{(0,+\infty)}(s)}{1+s^{2}}\leq 1,\quad\text{ for every }s\in\mathbb{R}

and let us define
\zeta_{n}(s):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\zeta_{n}(s)=\int_{0}^{s}\alpha_{n}(r){\mathrm{d}}r,\quad s\in\mathbb{R}.

Then, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\zeta_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
nondecreasing function whose derivative has compact support so that we can plug it into (
4.49
) in place of
\zeta
and we see that

(4.50)

|{\mathrm{D}}\,(\zeta_{n}\circ F^{\varepsilon}_{\nu})|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}(\mu)\leq\zeta_{n}^{\prime}(F^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mu))\sqrt{2F^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(\mu)}\quad\text{ for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$}.

Observe that
\zeta_{n}(s)\to\arctan(s){\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{(0,+\infty)}
and
\zeta^{\prime}_{n}(s)\to\frac{{\raise 0.90417pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{(0,+\infty)}(s)}{1+s^{2}}
for every
s\in\mathbb{R}
and the r.h.s. of (
4.50
) is uniformly bounded. Using Theorem
2.3
(1)-(3) we can thus pass to the limit as
n\to+\infty
and we obtain

|{\mathrm{D}}(\vartheta\circ W_{\nu}^{\varepsilon})|_{\star,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{A}}}(\mu)\leq\vartheta^{\prime}(W_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(\mu))\quad\text{ for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$},

where
\vartheta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined as
\vartheta(s):=\arctan(s^{2}/2){\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{(0,+\infty)}(s)
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
. We can thus apply Lemma
2.11
and conclude that

(4.51)

|{\mathrm{D}}W^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq 1\quad\text{ $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ and for every $0<\varepsilon<1$ }.

Choosing
\varepsilon=1/k
, we have
\lim_{k\to+\infty}W_{\nu}^{1/k}(\mu)=W_{\nu}(\mu)

for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
;
using Theorem
2.3
(1)-(3), we obtain (
4.47
).
∎

The
proof of Theorem
4.10

then easily follows by Corollary
4.18
and Theorem

2.13
.

We conclude this section with a simple but useful density property,
which shows the possibility to use smaller algebra of cylinder
functions to operate in
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
.

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be a subset of
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

satisfying
the following property: for every
f\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there
exists
a sequence
f_{n}\in\mathscr{F}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

(4.52)

\sup_{n}\|f_{n}\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla f_{n}\|_{\infty}<\infty,\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f_{n}-f|+|\nabla(f_{n}-f)|\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu=0\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$}.

Then the algebra
\mathscr{A}\subset\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
generated by the set
of cylinder functions
\big\{\mathsf{L}_{f}:f\in\mathscr{F}\big\}
is dense in
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
and satisfies the strong
approximation property of Theorem
4.10
.

In particular the algebra
\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{b}\!\!\left({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)

generated by
\big\{\mathsf{L}_{f}:f\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big\}
is
strongly dense in
H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
and satisfies the approximation
property of Theorem
4.10
.

Proof.

Thanks to Theorem
4.10
and a simple diagonal argument, it
is sufficient to prove that for every cylinder function
F\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
there exists a sequence
F_{n}\in\mathscr{A}
such that

(4.53)

F_{n}\to F\text{ in }L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{n}-F)\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

In the case
F=\mathsf{L}_{f}

with
f\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
(
4.52
) and Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem
show that we can
find a sequence
f_{n}\in\mathscr{F}
such that, setting
F_{n}:=\mathsf{L}_{f_{n}}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|F_{n}-F|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(f_{n}(x)-f(x))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty,

\displaystyle\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{n}-F)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int|\nabla f_{n}(x)-\nabla f(x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(x)\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

Let us now consider a general
F=\psi\circ\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{f}}

as in (
4.2
), where
\boldsymbol{f}=(f_{1},\cdots,f_{N})
is a
vector of functions in
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\psi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. If we consider
\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}:=(1,f_{1},\dots,f_{N})
and
\tilde{\psi}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{N+1})
defined as

\tilde{\psi}(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}):=\psi(0)x_{0}-\psi(0)+\psi(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{N}),\quad(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N+1},

we have that
\tilde{\psi}(0)=0
and
\tilde{\psi}\circ\mathsf{L}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}}=F
. For this reason we can always suppose that
f_{1}\equiv 1
and
\psi(0)=0
.
It is also not restrictive to assume that
\psi
is a polynomial with
\psi(0)=0
:
in fact, setting
R:=\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{d},\,1\leq k\leq N}\Big(|f_{k}|+|\nabla f_{k}|\Big)
,
we can find a sequence of polynomials
(P_{h})_{h}
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
such
that

(4.54)

P_{h}(0)=0,\quad\sup_{|z|\leq R}|P_{h}(z)-\psi(z)|+|\nabla P_{h}(z)-\nabla\psi(z)|\to 0\quad\text{as }h\to\infty.

It follows that

F_{h}:=P_{h}\circ\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{f}}}
satisfies

(4.55)

\lim_{h\to\infty}\sup_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big(|F_{h}(\mu)-F(\mu)|+\|{\mathrm{D}}F_{h}[\mu]-{\mathrm{D}}F[\mu]\|_{\mu}\Big)=0.

Let us consider sequences
(f_{k,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,

k=1,\cdots,N
, approximating
f_{k}
as in (
4.52
).
In particular, there exists
R>0
such that

\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(|f_{k,n}|+|\nabla f_{k,n}|+|f_{k}|+|\nabla f_{k}|\Big)\leq R

for every
n\in\mathbb{N},\ k\in\{1,\cdots,N\}
.
If
\psi
is a polynomial in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with
\psi(0)=0
then
the function
F_{n}:=\psi\circ\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{f}}_{n}}
belongs
to
\mathscr{A}
(cf. Remark
4.3
), where
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{n}=(f_{1,n},f_{2,n},\dots,f_{N,n})
. Denoting by
L
the maximum of the Lipschitz constants of

\psi
and
\partial_{k}\psi

in the cube
[-R,R]^{N}
with respect to the
\infty
-norm, it is easy to see that

\displaystyle|F_{n}(\mu)-F(\mu)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\psi(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{f}}_{n}}(\mu))-\psi(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{f}}_{n}}(\mu)))\Big|\leq L\sup_{k}|\mathsf{L}_{f_{k,n}}(\mu)-\mathsf{L}_{f_{k}}(\mu)|\to 0,

\displaystyle\|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}[\mu]-{\mathrm{D}}F[\mu]\|_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\sum_{k}\Big(\partial_{k}\psi(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{f}}_{n}}(\mu))\nabla f_{k,n}-\partial_{k}\psi(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{f}}}(\mu))\nabla f_{k}\Big)\Big\|_{\mu}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k}\Big\|\partial_{k}\psi(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{f}}_{n}}(\mu))\nabla f_{k,n}-\partial_{k}\psi(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{f}}_{n}}(\mu))\nabla f_{k}\Big\|_{\mu}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k}\Big\|\Big(\partial_{k}\psi(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{f}}_{n}}(\mu))-\partial_{k}\psi(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{f}}}(\mu))\Big)\nabla f_{k}\Big\|_{\mu}

\displaystyle\leq L\sum_{k}\left(\Big\|\nabla f_{k,n}-\nabla f_{k}\Big\|_{\mu}+R\Big|\langle f_{k,n}-f_{k},\mu\rangle\Big|\right).

Both terms are uniformly bounded w.r.t.
\mu
and
n
, and
converge to
0
as
n\to\infty
. We deduce that (
4.53
) holds.
∎

Remark 4.20

(Polynomials)
.

If there exists a radius
R>0
such that

\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mu)\subset\overline{{\mathrm{B}}(0,R)}
for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
\mu

then we can also choose subsets
\mathscr{F}
of
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in
Proposition
4.19
.
An interesting example is provided by the collection
\mathscr{F}
of all
the polynomials.
In this case the algebra
\mathscr{A}
is the set of functionals

\mu\mapsto\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}P(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{\otimes k}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}),\quad P\text{ polynomial in }(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

5.
Calculus rules

We now show that the Cheeger energy can be expressed in terms of
an appropriate notion of (relaxed) Wasserstein gradient, also depending on

\mathfrak{m}
, which enjoys useful calculus rules.

Theorem 5.1

(
\mathfrak{m}
-Wasserstein differential)
.

For every
F\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})

there exists a unique vector field
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\in L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
(the
\mathfrak{m}
-Wasserstein differential of
F
)
such that for every sequence

F_{n}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying
(
4.16
)
we have

(5.1)

{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}\to{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\quad\text{strongly in }L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Moreover:

(a)

The map
F\mapsto{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
from
D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
to

L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is linear and for every

F,G\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
we have

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F,G)=\int{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)\cdot{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}G(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x),\quad\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F)=\int|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x),

where
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the quadratic form associated to
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(\cdot)
as in Remark
2.18
.

(b)

The map
F\mapsto(F,{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F)
is a linear isometric (thus
continuous) immersion of

H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
into
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})\times L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(c)

The graph of
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
in
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})\times L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is (weakly) closed:
for every sequence
F_{n}\in H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})

(5.3)

\left.\begin{aligned} F_{n}\rightharpoonup F&\text{ in }L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})\\
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}\rightharpoonup{\mbox{\boldmath$G$}}&\text{ in }L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\end{aligned}\right\}\quad\Rightarrow\quad F\in H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2}),\ {\mbox{\boldmath$G$}}={\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F.

Proof.

The proof uses well known arguments of the theory of quadratic
forms. If
F_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, is a sequence in
\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
, then
for every
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(5.4)

\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{m}-F_{n})=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{m})+\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}(F_{n})\Big)-\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}(F_{m}+F_{n})\Big).

If (
4.16
) holds, we can pass to the limit as
m,n\to\infty
,
observing that

\lim_{m,n\to\infty}\frac{1}{2}(F_{m}+F_{n})=F
,
and therefore by (
2.16
)

\liminf_{m,n\to\infty}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}(F_{m}+F_{n})\Big)\geq\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F)
;
we thus obtain

(5.5)

\limsup_{m,n\to\infty}\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{m}-F_{n})=\limsup_{m,n\to\infty}\frac{1}{4}\int|{\mathrm{D}}F_{m}(\mu,x)-{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x)\leq 0

which shows that
m\mapsto{\mathrm{D}}F_{m}
is a Cauchy sequence in

L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and therefore converges to some element
V
.

If
\tilde{F}_{n}
is another sequence satisfying (
4.16
), we can
use the identity

(5.6)

\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{n}-\tilde{F}_{n})=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{n})+\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}(\tilde{F}_{n})\Big)-\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}(F_{n}+\tilde{F}_{n})\Big)

and the same argument to conclude that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{n}-\tilde{F}_{n})=0
, so that the limit
V

is independent of the approximating sequence and we are authorized
to call it
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
.

Concerning claim (a),
the linearity of
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
follows immediately from the linearity
of
{\mathrm{D}}
as a map from
\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
to
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

If
F,G\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
and
(F_{n})_{n},(G_{n})_{n}\subset\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
are sequences satisfying (
4.16
) for
F
and
G
respectively, we can see that
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{n},G_{n})\to\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F,G)
; indeed

\displaystyle\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{n},G_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{n}+G_{n})-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{n})-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(G_{n}),

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{n}-G_{n})+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{n})+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(G_{n}).

Passing the first equality to the
\liminf_{n}
, the second one to the
\limsup_{n}
and using (
2.16
), we get that
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F_{n},G_{n})\to\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F,G)
. Passing then to the limit in (
4.15
)
we immediately see that

(5.7)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F,G)=\int{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)\cdot{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}G(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x)

which, together with (
2.61
), shows that
F\mapsto(F,{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F)
is an isometry
from

H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
into
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})\times L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
(claim (b)).

Claim (c) then follows by claim (b) and the fact that

H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
is a Hilbert space.
∎

Let us now collect a few properties of
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
, which follow by
the corresponding
metric versions of Theorem
2.3
and
the approximation property of Theorem
5.1
.

Proposition 5.2

(Calculus properties of
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
)
.

The
\mathfrak{m}
-Wasserstein differential satisfies the following properties:

(a)

(Minimal relaxed gradient and pointwise Lipschitz constant)
For every

F\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})

we have

(5.8)

\|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F[\mu]\|_{\mu}^{2}=\int|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star}^{2}(\mu)\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

In particular we have the

pointwise Rademacher property
:
for every
F\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})

(5.9)

\|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F[\mu]\|_{\mu}^{2}=\int|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\leq\big|{\mathrm{D}}F\big|^{2}(\mu)\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$},

and if
F\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)

(5.10)

\int|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\leq\int|{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

(b)

(Leibniz rule)
If
F,G\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})\cap D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
, then

H:=FG\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
and

(5.11)

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}H(\mu,x)=F(\mu){\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}G(\mu,x)+G(\mu){\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)\quad\text{for $\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}$-a.e.\penalty\ $(\mu,x)\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}$}.

(c)

(Locality)
If
F\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
then
for any
\mathscr{L}^{1}
-negligible Borel subset
N\subset\mathbb{R}
we have

(5.12)

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F[\mu]=0\quad\text{in }L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ on $F^{-1}(N)$}.

(d)

(Truncations) If
F_{j}\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
,
j=1,\cdots,J
,
then
also the functions

F_{+}:=\max(F_{1},\cdots,F_{J})\text{ and }F_{-}:=\min(F_{1},\cdots,F_{J})

belong to
D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})

and

(5.13)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{+}={\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{j}

\displaystyle\quad\text{$\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}$-a.e.\penalty\ on }\{(\mu,x)\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:F_{+}(\mu)=F_{j}(\mu)\},

(5.14)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{-}={\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{j}

\displaystyle\quad\text{$\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}$-a.e.\penalty\ on }\{(\mu,x)\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:F_{-}(\mu)=F_{j}(\mu)\}.

(e)

(Chain rule)
If
F\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
and
\phi\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(\mathbb{R})

then
\phi\circ F\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
and

(5.15)

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\phi\circ F)=\phi^{\prime}(F)\,{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\quad\text{$\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}$-a.e.\penalty\ on }{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Remark 5.3
.

Notice that the product in (
5.15
) is well defined since there
exists a
{\mathscr{L}}^{1}
-negligible Borel set
N\subset\mathbb{R}
such that

\phi
is differentiable in
\mathbb{R}\setminus N
and

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
vanishes
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. in
F^{-1}(N)
thanks to
the locality property (
5.12
).

Proof.

Claim (a) is an immediate consequence of the fact that
(
4.16
)
yields
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits F_{n}\to|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star}
strongly in
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})
; up to
extracting a suitable (not relabeled) subsequence we get
