\int|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}[\mu]|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\to|{\mathrm{D}}F|^{2}_{\star}(\mu)

for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
\mu
. On the other hand since by (
5.1
)
{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}\to{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
in
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}|^{2}\to|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F|^{2}
in
L^{1}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})
; indeed

\displaystyle\int\Big||{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}(\mu,x)|^{2}-|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)|^{2}\Big|\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x)

\displaystyle\leq\int\left((|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}|+|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F|)|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}-{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F|\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int(|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}|+|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F|)^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\cdot\|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}-{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})}

so that

(5.16)

\int\Big||{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}(\mu,x)|^{2}-|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)|^{2}\Big|\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x)\to 0\quad\text{ as }n\to+\infty.

Hence Fubini’s Theorem yields, up to extracting a suitable
subsequence,

(5.17)

\int|{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\to\int|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.$\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$}.

(
5.9
) and (
5.10
) then follows by the general
properties of the minimal relaxed gradients
(recall Remark
2.8
).

Claim (c) follows by (
2.8
) and (
5.8
).

Claim (d) is just a consequence of the locality property
(
5.12
).

Claim (e) is true if
\phi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
just by
passing to the limit in the corresponding formula
for a cylinder function.
In fact if
F_{n}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
is a sequence as in (
4.16
) and
(
5.1
) we have

(5.18)

{\mathrm{D}}(\phi\circ F_{n})=(\phi^{\prime}\circ F_{n}){\mathrm{D}}F_{n}\quad\text{in
}\mathcal{D}.

Since
\phi^{\prime}
is bounded and continuous we get

(5.19)

{\mathrm{D}}(\phi\circ F_{n})\to{\mbox{\boldmath$G$}}=(\phi^{\prime}\circ F){\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\quad\text{strongly in
}L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{as $n\to\infty$}.

Integrating w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}
and recalling (
5.8
) and Theorem
2.3
(7) we get

\int|{\mbox{\boldmath$G$}}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}=\int|\phi^{\prime}(F(\mu))|^{2}|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x)=\int|\phi^{\prime}(F(\mu))|\,|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}=\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(\phi\circ F)

so that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(\phi\circ F_{n})=\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(\phi\circ F).

We conclude by Theorem
5.1
that
{\mbox{\boldmath$G$}}=(\phi^{\prime}\circ F){\mathrm{D}}_{m}F
coincides with
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\phi\circ F)
.

Let us now consider the case of a general Lipschitz function
\phi
; by truncation and Claim
(d) it is not restrictive to assume that
\phi
is also bounded.
We can find a sequence
\phi_{n}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
such
that
\sup_{\mathbb{R}}|\phi_{n}|+|\phi_{n}^{\prime}|\leq L<\infty
,

\phi_{n}\to\phi
uniformly, and
\phi_{n}^{\prime}(x)\to\phi^{\prime}(x)

for every
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus N
for a Borel set
N
with
{\mathscr{L}}^{1}(N)=0
.
We have

(5.20)

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\phi_{n}\circ F)=\phi_{n}^{\prime}(F){\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ in }{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Setting
\tilde{N}:=\{(\mu,x)\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}:F(\mu)\in N\}
,
Fubini’s Theorem and the locality property (
5.12
) yield

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)=0
for
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}
-a.e.
(\mu,x)\in\tilde{N}
.
On the other hand
\phi_{n}^{\prime}(F(\mu))\to\phi^{\prime}(F(\mu))
for every

(\mu,x)\in\mathcal{D}\setminus\tilde{N}
; since
\phi_{n}^{\prime}
is uniformly
bounded, we deduce that

(5.21)

\phi_{n}^{\prime}(F){\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\to\phi^{\prime}(F){\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\quad\text{strongly in }L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

We conclude by Theorem
5.1
(b) that
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\phi\circ F)=\phi^{\prime}(F){\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
.

Claim (b) follows by claim (e); indeed, since
F,G\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})
, we can find a constant
M>0
such that

|F|(\mu)\leq M,\quad|G|(\mu)\leq M,\quad|F+G|(\mu)\leq M\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$}.

Let
\phi\in\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits(\mathbb{R})
be such that
\phi(x)=x^{2}
for every
x\in[-M-1,M+1]
; then we have

\displaystyle{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}FG

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}((F+G)^{2})-\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(F^{2})-\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(G^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\phi\circ(F+G))-\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\phi\circ F)-\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\phi\circ G)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\phi^{\prime}(F+G){\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(F+G)-\frac{1}{2}\phi^{\prime}(F){\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F-\frac{1}{2}\phi^{\prime}(G){\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}G

\displaystyle=(F+G){\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(F+G)-F{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F-G{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}G

\displaystyle=F{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}G+G{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F

for
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}
-a.e.
(\mu,x)\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

∎

Corollary 5.4
.

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
is a local Dirichlet form in
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})

[
13
, 3.1.1]

enjoying
\Gamma
-calculus with Carré du champs
\Gamma
given by

(5.22)

\Gamma(F,G)[\mu]:=\int{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)\cdot{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}G(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$}.

In particular, for every

F,G\in H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
we have

(5.23)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F,G)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Gamma(F,G)[\mu]\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)=\int{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)\cdot{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}G(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x),

\displaystyle\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Gamma(F,F)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)=\int|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,x).

Proof.

The fact that
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
is a Dirichlet form follows by the truncation
property
(
5.15
) with
\phi(r):=r\land 1
.
Since
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(1)=0
, the same property with
\phi(r)=|r|
also shows
that
\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
is local
(see
[
13
, Corollary 5.1.4]
).

Using the Leibniz rule (
5.11
) one can also easily show that
the
\Gamma
-operator (
5.22
) is the Carré du champ
associated to
\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}

[
13
, Definition 4.1.2]
.
∎

5.1.
Tangent bundle, residual differentials and relaxation

In general we cannot guarantee that

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F)
coincides with
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F)
if
F\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
, or,
equivalently, that
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F={\mathrm{D}}F
: this
property corresponds to the closability of
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
.
We can however investigate the relations between

{\mathrm{D}}F
and
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
:
two useful tools are represented by the
closure of the graph of
{\mathrm{D}}

and by the
collection of all the weak limits of Wasserstein differentials along vanishing sequences.

Definition 5.5

(Multivalued gradient)
.

We denote by
{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{G}}}}\subset L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})\times L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})

the closure of the linear space
\big\{(F,{\mathrm{D}}F):F\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)\big\}
.
The multivalued gradient
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}:H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})\rightrightarrows L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the operator whose graph is
{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{G}}}}
.

It is clear that
{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{G}}}}
is a closed vector subspace of

L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})\times L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which can also be obtained as the
weak closure of
\big\{(F,{\mathrm{D}}F):F\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)\big\}
. Thus

{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
if and only if there exists a sequence
F_{n}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
such that

(5.24)

F_{n}\to F\text{ in }L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m}),\quad{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}\rightharpoonup{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\text{ in }L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Definition 5.6

(Residual gradients)
.

The set of residual gradients
\mathrm{G}_{0}\subset L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})

is defined as

(5.25)

\displaystyle\mathrm{G}_{0}:=\Big\{{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in{}

\displaystyle L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d}):\text{
there exists }(F_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big):

\displaystyle F_{n}\to 0\text{ in }L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m}),\ {\mathrm{D}}F_{n}\rightharpoonup{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\text{ in }L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\Big\}.

The notion of residual gradient is known in the literature, see e.g.
[
30
, Section 1.2]
.
Notice that
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
is closable if and only if
\mathrm{G}_{0}
is
trivial and that
\mathrm{G}_{0}
contains all the vector fields that are limits of gradients of vanishing sequence of functions (see also Lemma
5.9
(1)).
A third important space is the
L^{2}
tangent bundle of
{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.
In the following, given a Borel map
{\mbox{\boldmath$G$}}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we denote, for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, by
{\mbox{\boldmath$G$}}[\mu]
the map
x\mapsto{\mbox{\boldmath$G$}}(\mu,x)
.

Definition 5.7
.

We denote by
\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})
the subspace of

L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
of vector fields
V
satisfying

(5.26)

{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu]\in\Tan_{\mu}{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$}.

Lemma 5.8
.

\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})
is a closed subspace of

L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
which is a
L^{\infty}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})

module:

(5.27)

\text{for every }{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}),\ H\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m}):\quad H{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}).

For every
F\in H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
(resp.
F\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
)
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\in\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})
(resp.
{\mathrm{D}}F\in\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})
).
Finally, if
\mathscr{C}\subset{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a countable
set dense in
{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with respect to the
Lipschitz norm
\|\zeta\|_{\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits}:=\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\zeta|+|\nabla\zeta|

and
\mathscr{L}
is a countable set dense in
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})
then
the set

(5.28)

\mathscr{T}=\operatorname{span}\Big\{H\nabla\zeta:H\in\mathscr{L},\ \zeta\in\mathscr{C}\Big\}\quad\text{is dense
in $\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})$}.

Proof.

Let
({\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})
strongly
converging to
V
in
L^{2}
; it is not restrictive to assume that
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}

are Borel maps satisfying
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}[\mu]\in\Tan_{\mu}{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}
for a
\mathfrak{m}
-negligible set of
{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.
Up to extracting a suitable subsequence, we can also assume that

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\|{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}-{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}<\infty
.
Applying Fubini’s Theorem it follows that

\int_{{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}}\Big(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}[\mu](x)-{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu](x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}<+\infty

so that there exists a
\mathfrak{m}
-negligible set
{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}\supset{\mathcal{N}}
such that

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}[\mu](x)-{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu](x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)<\infty\quad\text{for every }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime};

and this implies that
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}[\mu]\to{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu]
strongly in

L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so that

{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu]\in\Tan_{\mu}{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
.

(
5.27
) is obvious. Since for every
F=\mathsf{L}_{\phi}
,
\phi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}

{\mathrm{D}}F[\mu]=\nabla\phi\in\Tan_{\mu}{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
for every

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, it is immediate to check that
{\mathrm{D}}F\in\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})

for every cylinder function.
The closure property of
\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})
then yields the
analogous conclusion for the Wasserstein differential of
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
of a Sobolev function
F\in H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
.

Let us eventually consider (
5.28
): it is sufficient to prove
that any
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathscr{T}^{\perp}
belongs to

\big(\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})\big)^{\perp}
, where
\perp
denotes the orthogonal complement in the Hilbert space
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
If
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathscr{T}^{\perp}
is a Borel vector field, then

\int_{{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}}\Big(\int\langle\nabla\zeta,{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}(\mu,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)\,H(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)=0

for every
\zeta\in\mathscr{C},\ H\in\mathscr{L}
. Since
\mathscr{L}
is dense in
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})
we have for every
\zeta\in\mathscr{C}

\int\langle\nabla\zeta,{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}(\mu,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=0\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$}

Since
\mathscr{C}
is countable, we can find a
\mathfrak{m}
-negligible set

{\mathcal{N}}\subset{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
such that

\int\langle\nabla\zeta,{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}(\mu,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=0\quad\text{for every $\zeta\in\mathscr{C}$ and every $\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}$}

which shows that
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu]\in\Big(\Tan_{\mu}{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big)^{\perp}
for
every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}
, so that for every
{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}\in\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})

\displaystyle\int\langle{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}(\mu,x),{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}(\mu,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu](x),{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}[\mu](x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)=0.\qed

Let us collect a few simple properties of
\mathrm{G}_{0}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\mathrm{G}_{0}
be as in (
5.25
).

(1)

\mathrm{G}_{0}
is a closed subspace of
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and coincides with the set

(5.29)

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}0=\big\{{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d}):(0,{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}})\in{\mathrm{G}}\big\}.

(2)

For every
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
there exists a sequence

F_{n}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big),\ n\in\mathbb{N},\ \text{such that}

(5.30)

F_{n}\to 0\text{ in }L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m}),\ {\mathrm{D}}F_{n}\to{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\text{ strongly in }L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Every element
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
is therefore characterized by the
property

(5.31)

\forall\,\varepsilon>0\ \ \exists\,F\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big):\quad\|F\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})}\leq\varepsilon,\quad\|{\mathrm{D}}F-{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\varepsilon.

(3)

\mathrm{G}_{0}
satisfies the locality property

(5.32)

\text{for every }{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0},\ H\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m}):\quad H{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}.

Proof.

We have already observed that
{\mathrm{G}}
is a closed vector space,
coinciding with the weak closure of
\big\{(F,{\mathrm{D}}F):F\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)\big\}
; in view of (
5.24
), (
5.25
) precisely characterizes the
elements
V
for which
(0,{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}})\in{\mathrm{G}}
. Therefore the first two
claims are obvious.

Let us eventually prove the last claim.
We first consider the case when
H\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
.
If
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
we can find a sequence
F_{n}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
such
that
(
5.30
) holds.
Setting
G_{n}:=HF_{n}
, since
H
is bounded we clearly have
G_{n}\to 0
strongly in
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})
;
moreover, by the Leibniz rule we get

(5.33)

{\mathrm{D}}G_{n}=H{\mathrm{D}}F_{n}+F_{n}{\mathrm{D}}H\to H{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}

since
{\mathrm{D}}H\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
F_{n}\to 0

strongly in
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})
.
We deduce that
H{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
as well.

If now
H
is a function in
L^{\infty}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})
we can find by (
2.3
) a uniformly bounded sequence
H_{n}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
converging to
H

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. in
{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, so that
H_{n}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\to H{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}
in

L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Being
\mathrm{G}_{0}
a closed subspace and
H_{n}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
by the
previous step, we deduce that
H{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
.
∎

We now define

(5.34)

\mathrm{T}:=\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})\cap\mathrm{G}_{0}^{\perp}=\Big\{{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}):\langle{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0\ \text{for every
}{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}\Big\},

where
\perp
denotes the orthogonal complement in the Hilbert space
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We can now obtain our main structure result.

Theorem 5.10
.

For every
F\in H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
we have
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\in\mathrm{T}
and for every

{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
we have
the pointwise orthogonality property

(5.35)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)\cdot{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=0\quad\text{for
$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$}.

If
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F

then

{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}-{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
.
In particular for every
F\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)

{\mathrm{D}}F-{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
and for every
G\in H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})

(5.36)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\mu,x)\cdot{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}G(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x)\cdot{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}G(\mu,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$}.

Finally, for every
F\in H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
,
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
is the element of minimal
L^{2}
-norm in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
.

Proof.

Let us first observe that if
F_{n}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
satisfies (
5.30
)
and
\tilde{F}_{n}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
satisfies (
5.1
), we have

F_{n}+\tilde{F}_{n}\to F
strongly in
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})
, with

{\mathrm{D}}(F_{n}+\tilde{F}_{n})\to{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F+{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}
, so that the lower semicontinuity of the Cheeger energy with respect to
L^{2}
convergence yields together with (
5.2
) that

(5.37)

\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}(F)=\int|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}\leq\int|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F+{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}.

Since
V
is arbitrary in
\mathrm{G}_{0}
we deduce that

\int{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\cdot{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}=0\quad\text{for every }{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}.

Replacing
V
with
H{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}
,
H\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})
we get

(5.38)

\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\cdot{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)H(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)=0\quad\text{for every }{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0},\ H\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m}),

which yields (
5.35
).

If now
F_{n}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
converges strongly to
F
with
{\mathrm{D}}F\rightharpoonup{\mbox{\boldmath$G$}}
, selecting
\tilde{F}_{n}
as above, we have

F_{n}-\tilde{F}_{n}\to 0
strongly in
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})
and
{\mathrm{D}}(F_{n}-\tilde{F}_{n})\rightharpoonup{\mbox{\boldmath$G$}}-{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
weakly in
L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so that
{\mbox{\boldmath$G$}}-{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
.
By (
5.37
) we conclude that
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F
is the element
of minimal norm in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F={\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F+\mathrm{G}_{0}
.
∎

We can give a “pointwise” interpretation of the orthogonality
properties of the previous Theorem.
Let us select an orthonormal set
\mathrm{O}_{0}:=\{{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}\subset\mathcal{L}^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
dense in

\mathrm{G}_{0}
(we are thus assuming that
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}
are Borel vector fields
everywhere
defined).
Since

\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)=1

we deduce that there exists a
\mathfrak{m}
-negligible set
{\mathcal{N}}\subset{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
such that

(5.39)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)<\infty\quad\text{for every
}n\in\mathbb{N},\ \mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}.

We thus define
\mathrm{G}_{0}[\mu]:=\overline{\mathrm{span}\{{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}[\mu]:n\in\mathbb{N}\}}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}
and

\mathrm{T}[\mu]:=\big(\mathrm{G}_{0}[\mu]\big)^{\perp}\cap\Tan_{\mu}{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, where here
\perp
denotes the orthogonal complement in the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
F\in H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(1)

V
belongs to

\mathrm{G}_{0}
if and only if, for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
\mu
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu]\in\mathrm{G}_{0}[\mu]
.

(2)

V
belongs to

\mathrm{T}
if and only if, for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
\mu
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu]\in\mathrm{T}[\mu]
.

(3)

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F[\mu]\in\mathrm{T}[\mu]

for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
\mu
.

(4)

If
F\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
then,
for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
,

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F[\mu]
is the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu)
-orthogonal projection of

{\mathrm{D}}F[\mu]
on
\mathrm{T}[\mu]
.

Proof.

If
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
we can write
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}=\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{N}
in

L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})

where
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{N}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}u_{n}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}
is the orthogonal projection of

V
on the space generated by
\{{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{1},\cdots,{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{N}\}
, with
u_{n}:={\langle}V,V_{n}{\rangle}
.
Clearly
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{N}[\mu]\in\mathrm{G}_{0}[\mu]
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}
.
Moreover we can find a subsequence, not relabeled, and a

\mathfrak{m}
-negligible set
{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}\supset{\mathcal{N}}
such that

{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{N}[\mu]\to{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu]
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
, so that
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu]\in\mathrm{G}_{0}[\mu]
for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
.

Let now
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in L^{2}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a vector field such that

{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu]\in\mathrm{G}_{0}[\mu]
for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. Since
\mathrm{G}_{0}

is a closed subspace, in order to show that
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
it is
sufficient to prove that the scalar product with
every element
{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}^{\perp}
vanishes.

If
{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}^{\perp}
then for every
H\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\mathfrak{m})

and every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we get

\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}\cdot{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)H(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)=0,

since
H{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}\in\mathrm{G}_{0}
by (
5.32
).
Being
H
arbitrary, we find that
there exists a
\mathfrak{m}
-negligible set
{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime\prime}\subset{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}[\mu]\cdot{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}[\mu]\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu=0\quad\text{for every }n\in\mathbb{N},\ \mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime\prime},

so that
{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}[\mu]\in\big(\mathrm{G}_{0}[\mu]\big)^{\perp}
for

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.
We then deduce that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}[\mu]\cdot{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu]\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu=0\quad\text{for $\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.\penalty\ $\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$},

and therefore

\langle{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}\cdot{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)=0.

The previous argument also shows that a vector field
V
belongs
to
\mathrm{G}_{0}^{\perp}
if and only if
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu]\in\big(\mathrm{G}_{0}[\mu])^{\perp}

for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. This fact, together with the very
definition of
\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})
(
5.26
), yields claim (2).

Claim (3) just follows by Theorem

5.10
, since
(
5.35
) shows that, for every
F\in H^{1,2}(\mathbb{W}_{2})
,

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F[\mu]\in\mathrm{T}[\mu]

for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.

If
F\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
, combining claim 1 and Theorem
5.10
, we see that
{\mathrm{D}}F[\mu]-{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F[\mu]\in\mathrm{G}_{0}[\mu]\subset\big(\mathrm{T}[\mu]\big)^{\perp}

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e., so that

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F[\mu]
is the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})
-orthogonal projection of
{\mathrm{D}}F[\mu]
on
\mathrm{T}[\mu]
, as stated in Claim (4).
∎

We can now interpret the above results in terms of the nonsmooth
tangent and cotangent structures introduced and developed by Gigli in

[
25
]
. Since we are in the Hilbertian case, we can identify
the cotangent module
L^{2}(T^{*}{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})
and dual tangent module
L^{2}(T{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})
with the Hilbert space
\mathrm{T}
defined by
(
5.34
). Let us report a useful characterization of the cotangent module
L^{2}(T^{*}X)

[
26
, Theorem 4.1.1]
for a general metric measure space
(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
be a metric measure space. Then there exists a unique pair
((\mathcal{M},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}},\cdot_{\mathcal{M}},|\cdot|_{\mathcal{M}}),\mathsf{diff})
such that
(\mathcal{M},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}},\cdot_{\mathcal{M}},|\cdot|_{\mathcal{M}})
is a
L^{2}(X,\mathfrak{m})
-normed
L^{\infty}(X,\mathfrak{m})
module (cf.
[
26
, Definition 3.1.1]
) and
\mathsf{diff}:D^{1,2}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})\to\mathcal{M}
is a linear operator such that

(i)

|\mathsf{diff}(f)|_{\mathcal{M}}=|{\mathrm{D}}f|_{\star}

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. in
X
for every
f\in D^{1,2}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
.

(ii)

\mathcal{M}
is generated by
\left\{\mathsf{diff}(f):f\in D^{1,2}(X,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})\right\}
.

Uniqueness is intended in the following sense: if
((\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\|\cdot\|_{\tilde{\mathcal{M}}},\cdot_{\tilde{\mathcal{M}}},|\cdot|_{\tilde{\mathcal{M}}}),\tilde{\mathsf{diff}})
is another pair with the above properties, then there exists a unique module isomorphism
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{M}\to\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
such that
\tilde{\mathsf{diff}}=\mathcal{J}\circ\mathsf{diff}
.

We thus have the following result.

Theorem 5.13
.

There exists a unique module isomorphism
\mathcal{I}:\mathrm{T}\to L^{2}(T^{*}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cong L^{2}(T{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})
such that
\mathcal{I}\circ{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
coincides with the abstract differential operator taking values in
L^{2}(T^{*}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
as in
[
25
, Definition 2.2.2]
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
\mathrm{T}
(with an appropriate module structure) and the map
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
satisfy the properties listed in Theorem
5.12
.

If as
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{T}}
we take the
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
norm, it is clear that
(\mathrm{T},\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{T}})
is a Banach space, being closed by Lemma
5.8
. The pointwise product
\cdot_{\mathrm{T}}:L^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})\times\mathrm{T}\to\mathrm{T}
is well defined by (
5.27
) and (
5.32
), bilinear and associative in
L^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
by definition. Defining the pointwise norm
|\cdot|_{\mathrm{T}}
as the map sending
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{T}
to
\|{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}[\mu]\|_{\mu}
, we immediately have that
\|{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\|_{\mathrm{T}}=\||{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}|_{\mathrm{T}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})}
and
|H\cdot_{\mathrm{T}}{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}|_{\mathrm{T}}=|H||{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}|_{\mathrm{T}}

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{T}
and every
H\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
. This shows that
(\mathrm{T},\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{T}},\cdot_{\mathrm{T}},|\cdot|_{\mathrm{T}})
is a
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
-normed
L^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
module. Taking as
\mathsf{diff}
the map
{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}:D^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})\to\mathrm{T}
, we see that it is well defined and linear by Theorem
5.1
and Theorem
5.10
. Property (i) of Theorem
5.12
follows by (
5.8
). Finally property (ii) of Theorem
5.12
, meaning that (
[
26
, Definition 3.1.13]
)
\mathrm{T}
coincides with the
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{T}}
-closure of

\mathrm{T}_{0}:=\operatorname{span}\left\{H{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F:H\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m}),\,F\in D^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})\right\},

follows by (
5.28
) and the definition of
\mathrm{T}
. Indeed, let
\mathscr{L}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
be a dense subset of
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
and
\mathscr{C}
be a dense subset of
{\mathrm{C}}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with respect to the Lipschtiz norm as in Lemma
5.8
. If
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathrm{T}
, in particular
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\Tan({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}})
so that we can find by (
5.28
) numbers
(N_{n})_{n}\subset\mathbb{N}
,
(\{\alpha_{n}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{n}})_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}
and functions
(\{H_{n}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{n}})_{n}\subset\mathscr{L}
,
(\{\zeta_{n}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{n}})_{n}\subset\mathscr{C}
such that the sequence

{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}(\mu,x):=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{n}}\alpha_{n}^{i}H_{n}^{i}(\mu)\nabla\zeta_{n}^{i}(x),\quad(\mu,x)\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\,n\in\mathbb{N}

converges to
V
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Consider now the functions
F_{n}^{i}:=\mathsf{L}_{\zeta_{n}^{i}}
and the sequence

{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{\prime}_{n}(\mu,x):=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{n}}\alpha_{n}^{i}H_{n}^{i}(\mu){\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}^{i},\quad(\mu,x)\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\,n\in\mathbb{N}.

It is clear that
({\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{\prime}_{n})_{n}\subset\mathrm{T}_{0}
; by Theorem
5.11
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{\prime}_{n}
is the orthogonal projection of
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{n}
on
\mathrm{T}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, so that
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}^{\prime}_{n}
converges to
V
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Theorem
5.12
gives thus the existence of a unique module isomorphism
\mathcal{I}:\mathrm{T}\to L^{2}(T^{*}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

Finally, notice that
L^{2}(T^{*}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cong L^{2}(T{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})
since
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})
is infinitesimally Hilbertian by Corollary
4.11
(see also
[
26
, Theorem 4.3]
).
∎

5.2.
Examples

Isometric embedding of Euclidean Sobolev spaces

Let
\Omega
be a Lipschitz bounded open set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For every
\omega\in\Omega
let us consider the Dirac mass
\delta_{\omega}

concentrated at
\omega
. The map
\iota:\omega\mapsto\delta_{\omega}
is an isometry
between
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\iota(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Setting
\mathfrak{m}:=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathscr{L}}^{d}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\Omega
we easily see that

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})
is isomorphic to

H^{1,2}(\Omega)
.

In this case only Dirac masses are involved and cylinder functions are of the form
F(\delta_{\omega})=\psi({\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\omega))
, so that
the Wasserstein gradient reduces to the usual gradient of

\psi\circ{\boldsymbol{\phi}}
.

Another isometric embedding is also possible:
we fix a reference measure
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
symmetric
w.r.t. the origin
and we consider the map
\iota:\Omega\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
given by

(5.40)

\iota(\omega):=\lambda(\cdot-\omega)=(\mathsf{t}_{\omega})_{\sharp}\lambda,\quad\mathsf{t}_{\omega}(x):=x+\omega,\quad\omega\in\Omega.

To every function
F:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
corresponds a map
\hat{F}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

(5.41)

\hat{F}(\omega):=F((\mathsf{t}_{\omega})_{\sharp}\lambda).

In the case of a cylinder function as in (
4.2
) we get

(5.42)

\hat{F}(\omega)=\psi\Big(\int\phi_{1}(x+\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda(x),\cdots,\int\phi_{N}(x+\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda(x)\Big)=\psi\Big(\phi_{1}*\lambda(\omega),\cdots,\phi_{N}*\lambda(\omega)\Big).

In this case (identifying
\iota(\omega)
with
\omega
) we have

(5.43)

{\mathrm{D}}F(\omega,x)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial\psi_{j}(\phi_{1}*\lambda(\omega),\cdots,\phi_{N}*\lambda(\omega))\nabla\phi_{j}(x)

and

(5.44)

\|{\mathrm{D}}F[\omega]\|_{\omega}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial\psi_{j}(\phi_{1}*\lambda(\omega),\cdots,\phi_{N}*\lambda(\omega))\nabla\phi_{j}(x+\omega)\Big|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda(x).

On the other hand,
\iota
is an isometry of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so that
the space
H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})
is still isomorphic to

H^{1,2}(\Omega)
.
It follows that the
\mathfrak{m}
-Wasserstein gradient of
F
is

(5.45)

{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\omega,x)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial\psi_{j}(\phi_{1}*\lambda(\omega),\cdots,\phi_{N}*\lambda(\omega))\nabla\phi_{j}*\lambda(\omega)

independent of
x
and the minimal relaxed gradient is

(5.46)

|{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}F|^{2}_{\star}(\omega)=\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial\psi_{j}(\phi_{1}*\lambda(\omega),\cdots,\phi_{N}*\lambda(\omega))\nabla\phi_{j}*\lambda(\omega)\Big|^{2}

Gaussian distributions

Let now
\kappa=N(\omega,\Sigma):=(\det(2\pi\Sigma))^{-1/2}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\omega,\Sigma^{-1}\omega\rangle}{\mathscr{L}}^{d}
be a Gaussian
measure with mean
\omega
and covariance matrix
\Sigma\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(d)
, the space of symmetric and positive definite

d\times d
-matrices; we
consider the set

(5.47)

\mathcal{N}^{d}:=\Big\{\mathcal{N}(\omega,\Sigma):\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ \Sigma\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(d)\Big\},

endowed with the Wasserstein distance and a finite positive Borel measure

\mathfrak{m}
concentrated on
\mathcal{N}^{d}
.
Since

(5.48)

W^{2}_{2}(N(\omega_{1},\Sigma_{1}),N(\omega_{2},\Sigma_{2}))=|\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}|^{2}+\mathrm{tr}\Sigma_{1}+\mathrm{tr}\Sigma_{2}-2\mathrm{tr}\Big(\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\Big)^{1/2},

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})
is isometric to

H^{1,2}(U,{\sf d},\hat{\mathfrak{m}})

where
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\overline{\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(d)}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

endowed with the distance
{\sf d}
induced by the formula
(
5.48
) and
\hat{\mathfrak{m}}
is the measure induced by
\mathfrak{m}
.

The closable case

Following
[
17
]
(here in the simpler setting of the Euclidean space, but see Section
6.2
below),
we assume that
\mathfrak{m}
has no atoms and the following integration by
parts formula: for every
G\in\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)

and
w\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists
{\mathrm{D}}^{*}_{w}G\in L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
such that for every
F\in\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)
it holds

\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x)\cdot w(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right)G(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu)=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}{\mathrm{D}}^{*}_{w}G(\mu)F(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}(\mu).

This equality implies that
\mathrm{G}_{0}=\{0\}
i.e. that
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
is
closable. We notice that the measure
\mathfrak{m}
induced by the immersion in
the space of delta measures considered at the beginning of this section
satisfies the integration by parts formula above (see also Example 5.4
in
[
17
]
). In
[
17
]
, in case the base
space is a compact Riemannian manifold, are reported important
examples of measures
\mathfrak{m}
satisfying the (Riemannian analogue of the)
integration by parts formula: the normalized mixed Poisson measure
(Example 5.11 in
[
17
]
and
[
1
,
41
]
), the
entropic measure over
\mathbb{S}^{1}
(Example 5.15 in

[
17
]
and
[
50
]
, see also the multidimensional
case
[
47
]
) and the Malliavin–Shavgulidze image measure (Example 5.18 in
[
17
]
and
[
33
]
).

6.
Extensions to Riemannian manifolds and Hilbert spaces

The aim of this Section is to extend the density result stated in
Theorem
4.10
from the finite dimensional and flat space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to Riemannian manifolds and (possibly infinite dimensional) Hilbert spaces.
Our first step deals with manifold embedded in some Euclidean space

\mathbb{R}^{d}
and in fact we will consider more general closed subsets of

\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

6.1.
Intrinsic Wasserstein spaces on closed subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}

In this subsection we denote by
\varrho
the Euclidean distance on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
still denotes the subset of Borel probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite second
\varrho
-moment and
W_{2}
is the Wasserstein distance on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
induced by
\varrho
.

We assume that
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a closed set and that
\sigma
is a
distance on
C
such that
(C,\sigma)
is a complete and separable
metric space
and

(6.1)

\varrho(x_{1},x_{2})\leq\sigma(x_{1},x_{2})\leq\varrho_{C,\ell}(x_{1},x_{2})\quad\text{ for every }x_{1},x_{2}\in C,

where
\varrho_{C,\ell}
is defined as in (
2.65
) with respect
to the distance
{\sf d}:=\varrho
.
Since the topology induced by
\sigma

is stronger than the Euclidean topology and they are both Polish
topologies,
the Borel sets of
(C,\sigma)
coincide with the Borel sets of
C
as
a subset of the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This means that every Borel
probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

with support contained in
C
can be identified with a Borel
probability measure in
(C,\sigma)
. Conversely any probability
measure on
(C,\sigma)
extends to a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We
can thus denote unambiguously by
\mathcal{P}(C)
the set of Borel
probability measures on
C
and by
\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C)
the elements
of
\mathcal{P}(C)
with finite second
\sigma
-moment.

\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C)

can be identified with the subset of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mu)\subset C,\quad\int_{C}\sigma^{2}(x_{0},x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)<+\infty\text{ for some }x_{0}\in C\right\}.

We will denote by
\iota:C\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
the inclusion map;
{\boldsymbol{\iota}}:\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is the corresponding continuous injection given by

{\boldsymbol{\iota}}(\mu):=\iota_{\sharp}\mu
, which may be identified with the
inclusion map of
\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C)
into
\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Since
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(C),W_{2,\sigma})
is a complete and separable
metric space and the topology induced by
W_{2,\sigma}
is stronger
than the topology induced by
W_{2}
, we deduce that

\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C)
is a Lusin (and therefore Borel) subset of

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

If
\mathfrak{m}
is a finite and positive Borel measure on
\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C)
,

{\boldsymbol{\iota}}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}
is
the Borel measure in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
which is
concentrated on
\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C)
and satisfies
{\boldsymbol{\iota}}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}(Z)=\mathfrak{m}(Z\cap\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C))

for every Borel set
Z\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

In a similar way, if
F:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is a Borel (or

{\boldsymbol{\iota}}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}
- measurable) map, we will
set
{\boldsymbol{\iota}}^{*}F:=F\circ{\boldsymbol{\iota}}:\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C)\to\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 6.1
.

We have
H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C),W_{2,\sigma},\mathfrak{m})\cong H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},{\boldsymbol{\iota}}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m})

with equal minimal relaxed gradient, meaning that

(6.2)

|{\mathrm{D}}({\boldsymbol{\iota}}^{*}F)|_{\star}={\boldsymbol{\iota}}^{*}\left(|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star}\right)\text{
for every }F\in H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},{\boldsymbol{\iota}}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}).

In particular

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C),W_{2,\sigma},\mathfrak{m})
is a Hilbert space and the algebra of
cylinder functions
{\boldsymbol{\iota}}^{*}\big(\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{b}\big({\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)\big)
is dense in

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C),W_{2,\sigma},\mathfrak{m})
in the sense of (
4.16
).

Proof.

We want to apply Theorem
2.24

where
X:=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

{\sf d}:=W_{2}
,
Y:=\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C)
, and
\delta:=W_{2,\sigma}
.
The first assumption of Condition (A),
{\boldsymbol{\iota}}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\setminus\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C))=0
, is clearly
satisfied by construction.

In order to prove (
2.71
) we consider a
W_{2}
-Lipschitz curve

\mu:[0,\ell]\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
parametrized by the
W_{2}
-arc-length
such that
\mu_{s}\in\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C)
for
{\mathscr{L}}^{1}
-a.e.
s\in[0,\ell]
.
Since the map
\mu
is continuous in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
C
is a
closed set, and
\mu_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus C)=0
for

{\mathscr{L}}^{1}
-a.e.
s\in[0,\ell]
, we conclude that
\mu_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus C)=0
for every

s\in[0,\ell]
.

By
[
4
, Theorem 8.2.1, Theorem 8.3.1]
)
there exists a measure
{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathrm{C}}([0,\ell];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
concentrated on absolutely continuous curves such that
(\mathsf{e}_{t})_{\sharp}({\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}})=\mu_{t}
for every
t\in[0,\ell]
and

(6.3)

\int|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\gamma)=\int|\dot{\gamma}|^{2}_{\varrho}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\gamma)=1\quad\text{for
a.e.\penalty\ }t\in[0,\ell].

Let us also consider the function
\zeta(x):=\mathrm{dist}(x,C)\land 1
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
where
\mathrm{dist}(x,C):=\min_{z\in C}\varrho(x,z)
.
\zeta
is a bounded
Lipschitz function which vanishes precisely on
C
.
Fubini’s Theorem yields

\displaystyle\int\Big(\int_{0}^{\ell}\zeta(\gamma(t))\,{\mathrm{d}}t\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\ell}\int\zeta({\sf e}_{t}(\gamma))\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}t=\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\zeta\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}\,{\mathrm{d}}t=0

since
\int\zeta(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}=0
for
{\mathscr{L}}^{1}
-a.e.
t\in(0,\ell)
.

It follows that
\int_{0}^{\ell}\zeta(\gamma(t))\,{\mathrm{d}}t=0
for
\eta
-a.e.
\gamma
, so
that the set of
t\in[0,\ell]
for which

\gamma(t)\in C
is dense in
[0,\ell]
. Being
C
closed,
we conclude that
\gamma
takes values in
C
for

\eta
-a.e.
\gamma
.

We can now estimate the
W_{2,\sigma}
distance between the two measures

\mu_{t_{0}}
and
\mu_{t_{1}}
, where
0\leq t_{0}<t_{1}\leq\ell
:

\displaystyle W^{2}_{2,\sigma}(\mu_{t_{0}},\mu_{t_{1}})

\displaystyle\leq\int\sigma^{2}(\gamma(t_{0}),\gamma(t_{1}))\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\leq\int\Big(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}|\dot{\gamma}|_{\sigma}(s)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\Big)^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}|\dot{\gamma}|_{\varrho}(s)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\Big)^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\leq(t_{1}-t_{0})\int\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}|\dot{\gamma}|_{\varrho}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}s\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=(t_{1}-t_{0})\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int|\dot{\gamma}|_{\varrho}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=(t_{1}-t_{0})^{2},

where we have used that
(\mathsf{e}_{t_{0}},\mathsf{e}_{t_{1}})_{\sharp}{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{t_{0}},\mu_{t_{1}})
, (
6.1
) and Remark
2.23
to say that
|\dot{\gamma}|_{\varrho}(s)=|\dot{\gamma}|_{\sigma}(s)
.

Choosing
t_{0}\in[0,\ell]
such that
\mu_{t_{0}}\in\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C)
we deduce that
\mu_{t_{1}}\in\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C)
as well for every
t_{1}\in[0,\ell]
. This
concludes the proof of property (A).

Condition (B) corresponds to

(6.4)

W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\leq W_{2,\sigma}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\leq(W_{2})_{Y,\ell}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\quad\text{ for every }\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in Y=\mathcal{P}_{2,\sigma}(C),

where
(W_{2})_{Y,\ell}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
is defined as in (
2.65
)
with
W_{2}
in place of
{\sf d}
. The first inequality immediately
follows by (
6.1
); to prove the second one,
we use
(
2.66
) and
the above estimate with
t_{0}=0
and
t_{1}=\ell

for a
W_{2}
-Lipschitz curve
\mu:[0,\ell]\to Y
such that

|\dot{\mu}|_{W_{2}}=1
a.e. in
[0,\ell]
with
\mu|_{t=0}=\mu_{0}
and

\mu|_{t=\ell}=\mu_{1}
.
Taking the infimum w.r.t.
\ell

we obtain (
6.4
).
∎

6.2.
Wasserstein Sobolev space on complete Riemannian
manifolds

In this subsection, we will briefly discuss the case of the Sobolev
space
H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})
where

(\mathbb{M},{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}})
is a smooth and complete Riemannian manifold endowed with
the canonical Riemannian distance
{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}
(inducing the Wasserstein distance
W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}}
) and
\mathfrak{m}
is a finite and positive Borel measure on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})
.
We will denote by
\mathscr{A}
the unital algebra generated by
\big\{\mathsf{L}_{f}:f\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{M})\big\}
.

Theorem 6.2
.

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})
is a Hilbert space and the algebra
\mathscr{A}
is
(strongly) dense:
for every
F\in H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})

there exists a sequence
F_{n}\in\mathscr{A}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(6.5)

F_{n}\to F,\quad\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits(F_{n})\to|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star}\quad\text{strongly in }L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),\mathfrak{m}).

Proof.

By Nash isometric embedding Theorem
[
36
]

we can find a dimension
d
,
and an isometric embedding

\jmath:\mathbb{M}\to\jmath(\mathbb{M})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
On
M:=\jmath(\mathbb{M})
we can define
the (Riemannian) metric
{\sf d}_{M}
inherited by
{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}
:

{\sf d}_{M}(\jmath(x),\jmath(y))={\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}(x,y)
so that
\jmath
is an
isometry and

(M,{\sf d}_{M})
is a complete and separable metric space.
We denote by
{\boldsymbol{\jmath}}:=\jmath_{\sharp}
the corresponding isometry between

(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}})
and
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(M),W_{2,{\sf d}_{M}})
and
we also set
\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}:={\boldsymbol{\jmath}}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}
which is a positive and finite Borel measure on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(M)
.

It is clear that the
map
{\boldsymbol{\jmath}}^{*}:F\mapsto F\circ{\boldsymbol{\jmath}}
induces a linear isometric isomorphism
between

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(M),W_{2,{\sf d}_{M}},\tilde{\mathfrak{m}})
and

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})
.

Since
\mathbb{M}
is complete and
\jmath
is an embedding,
M
is a closed
subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
{\sf d}_{M}
induces on
M
the relative topology
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since
\jmath
is isometric, we also have

(6.6)

\varrho(y_{1},y_{2})\leq{\sf d}_{M}(y_{1},y_{2})=\varrho_{M,\ell}(y_{1},y_{2})\quad\text{ for every }y_{1},y_{2}\in M,

where
\varrho_{M,\ell}
is as in (
2.65
) and
\varrho
denotes the Euclidean distance on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

As in Section
6.1
,
we can introduce the inclusion map
\iota:M\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the
corresponding

{\boldsymbol{\iota}}=\iota_{\sharp}:\mathcal{P}_{2,{\sf d}_{M}}(M)\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
By Theorem
6.1

we have that
the map
{\boldsymbol{\iota}}^{*}:F\mapsto F\circ{\boldsymbol{\iota}}
provides a linear isometric
isomorphism
between
H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},{\boldsymbol{\iota}}_{\sharp}\tilde{\mathfrak{m}})
and

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2,{\sf d}_{M}}(M),W_{2,{\sf d}_{M}},\tilde{\mathfrak{m}})
satisfying (
6.2
);
we conclude that the map

{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}:={\boldsymbol{\jmath}}^{*}\circ{\boldsymbol{\iota}}^{*}=({\boldsymbol{\iota}}\circ{\boldsymbol{\jmath}})^{*}

is a linear isometric
isomorphism
between
H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m})

(notice that
{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{\sharp}={\boldsymbol{\iota}}_{\sharp}\circ{\boldsymbol{\jmath}}_{\sharp}
) and

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}}(M),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})

satisfying

(6.7)

|{\mathrm{D}}({\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}F)|_{\star}={\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}\left(|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star}\right)\text{
for every }F\in H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}).

This property in particular yields the Hilbertianity of
H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})
.

In order to prove that
\mathscr{A}
is dense in

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})
we consider
the algebra
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
generated by
\big\{\mathsf{L}_{\tilde{f}}:\tilde{f}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big\}
; Proposition

4.19
shows that
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
is strongly dense in

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{m}})
, so that
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}:={\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}(\tilde{\mathscr{A}})

is strongly dense in
H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})
.

\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
is generated by functions of the form
{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}\mathsf{L}_{\tilde{f}}
,

\tilde{f}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Since

{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}\mathsf{L}_{\tilde{f}}(\mu)=\mathsf{L}_{\tilde{f}}({\boldsymbol{\kappa}}(\mu))=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\tilde{f}(\kappa(x))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\quad\text{for every $\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}}(\mathbb{M})$},

where
\kappa=\iota\circ\jmath
,
we see that
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
is generated by functions of the form
\mathsf{L}_{\tilde{f}\circ\kappa}
, so
that
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\subset\mathscr{A}
and a fortiori
\mathscr{A}
is strongly dense in

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})
as well.

To prove (
6.5
) (involving the asymptotic Lipschitz constants
of functions in
\mathscr{A}
with respect to the Riemannian metric) we
observe that for every
\tilde{F}\in\tilde{\mathscr{A}}

[
44
, Lemma 3.1.14]

(6.8)

{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}\tilde{F}\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\subset\mathscr{A},\quad{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{W_{2}}\tilde{F})\geq\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}}}{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}\tilde{F}.

Let now
F={\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}\tilde{F}\in H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})

with
\tilde{F}\in H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{m}})
;
there exists a sequence
\tilde{F}_{n}\in\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
such that

\tilde{F}_{n}\to\tilde{F},\quad\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{W_{2}}\tilde{F}_{n}\to|{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{F}|_{\star}\quad\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}).

Applying the linear isometric isomorphism
{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}
,
we deduce that the sequence
{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}F_{n}\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
satisfies

(6.9)

{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}\tilde{F}_{n}\to F,\quad{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}\big(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{W_{2}}\tilde{F}_{n}\big)\to{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}\big(|{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{F}|_{\star}\big)=|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star}\quad\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}}(\mathbb{M}),{\mathfrak{m}}).

Up to extracting a suitable (not relabelled) subsequence and using (
6.8
),
we can suppose that
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}}}{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}\tilde{F}_{n}

converges weakly in
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}})
to some
G\in L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}})
relaxed gradient of
F
.
(
6.8
) and (
6.9
) also yield

\int G^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}}}{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}F_{n})^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int\Big({\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}(\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{W_{2}}\tilde{F}_{n})\Big)^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m}=\int|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathfrak{m},

showing that
G=|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star}
and

\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}}}{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{*}F_{n}\to|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star}
strongly in
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}}(\mathbb{M}),\mathfrak{m})
.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

Arguing as in Section
4.1

it is immediate to see that the restriction of
\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2}
to
the algebra
\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})\right)
is a quadratic form
and coincides with the pre-Dirichlet forms considered in

[
50
,
47
,
17
,
18
]
.
If
\big(\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2},\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})\right))
is closable then

\big(\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}_{2},H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M}),W_{2,{\sf d}_{\mathbb{M}}},\mathfrak{m})\big)
is a
Dirichlet form which coincides
with the smallest closed extension of

\big(\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2},\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})\right))
, it satisfies the
so-called Rademacher property (see Proposition
5.2

and

[
17
]
) and it is quasi-regular (see Remark

2.19
).

In particular it is possible to improve the
result
[
17
, Theorem 2.10]
. Referring to the
notation and the formula enumeration of that paper,
one can immediately obtain that
Lipschitz functions belong to
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and the estimate
(2.16) holds just assuming that

\big(\mathsf{pC\kern-1.0ptE}_{2},\operatorname{FC}^{\infty}_{c}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})\right)\big)
is closable.

6.3.
Wasserstein Sobolev space on Hilbert spaces

In this last section we will consider the case of a separable Hilbert
space

(\mathbb{H},|\cdot|)
; as usual, the space
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})
will be endowed with the
Wasserstein distance
\mathbb{W}_{2}
induced by the Hilbertian norm of
\mathbb{H}
and
we will assume that
\mathfrak{m}
is a finite and positive Borel measure on

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})
.

We select a complete orthonormal system
E:=(e_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
the collection of maps

\pi_{d}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
, given by

(6.10)

\pi^{d}(x):=\big(\langle x,e_{1}\rangle,\cdots,\langle x,e_{d}\rangle\big).

The adjoint map
\pi^{d*}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{H}
is given by

(6.11)

\pi^{d*}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{d}):=\sum_{j=1}^{d}y_{j}\,e_{j}.

The map
\hat{\pi}^{d}:=\pi^{d*}\circ\pi^{d}
is the orthogonal projection
of
\mathbb{H}
onto
\mathrm{span}\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{d}\}
.
We say that a function
\phi:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
belongs to
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{H},E)
if it can be
written as

(6.12)

\phi:=\varphi\circ\pi^{d}\quad\text{for some }d\in\mathbb{N},\ \varphi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

If
\phi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{H},E)
then it belongs to
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{H})
and its gradient
\nabla\phi
can be written as

(6.13)

\nabla\phi=\pi^{d*}\circ\nabla\varphi\circ\pi^{d},\quad\nabla\phi(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\partial_{j}\varphi(\pi^{d}(x))e_{j}.

We then consider the algebra
\operatorname{FC}^{1}_{b}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})\right)
generated by
\left\{\mathsf{L}_{\phi}:\phi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{H},E)\right\}
. For every
F\in\operatorname{FC}^{1}_{b}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})\right)
we can find
N\in\mathbb{N}
, a polynomial

\psi:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
and functions
\phi_{n}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{H},E)
,
n=1,\cdots,N
, such that

(6.14)

F(\mu)=(\psi\circ\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})(\mu).

As in (
4.4
) we can set

(6.15)

{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x):=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\partial_{n}\psi(\mathsf{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(\mu))\nabla\phi_{n}(x).

It is also easy to check that a function
F
belongs to

\operatorname{FC}^{1}_{b}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})\right)

if and only if there exists
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tilde{F}\in\operatorname{FC}^{1}_{b}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)
such that

(6.16)

F(\mu)=\tilde{F}(\pi^{d}_{\sharp}(\mu))\quad\text{for every }\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H}),

so that

(6.17)

{\mathrm{D}}F(\mu,x)=\pi^{d*}\Big({\mathrm{D}}\tilde{F}(\pi^{d}_{\sharp}\mu,\pi^{d}(x))\Big),\quad\|{\mathrm{D}}F[\mu]\|_{\mu}=\|{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{F}(\pi^{d}_{\sharp}\mu)\|_{\pi^{d}_{\sharp}\mu}.

By Proposition
4.9
and using (
6.17
)
it is not difficult to check that

(6.18)

\|{\mathrm{D}}F[\mu]\|_{\mu}=\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits F(\mu)\quad\text{for every }\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H}).

Adapting in an obvious way the definitions in (
4.14
) and (
4.15
) to
the Hilbertian framework, we have the following result.

Theorem 6.4
.

H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})
is a Hilbert space and the algebra
\operatorname{FC}^{1}_{b}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})\right)
is
(strongly) dense:
for every
F\in H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})

there exists a sequence
F_{n}\in\operatorname{FC}^{1}_{b}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})\right)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(6.19)

F_{n}\to F,\quad\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits(F_{n})\to|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star}\quad\text{strongly in }L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H}),\mathfrak{m}).

Proof.

Let us set
\mathscr{A}:=\operatorname{FC}^{1}_{b}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})\right)
; we use Theorem
2.13

and we want to prove that for every
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})
the
function

(6.20)

F(\mu):=W_{2}(\nu,\mu)\quad\text{satisfies}\quad|{\mathrm{D}}F|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq 1\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.}.

We split the proof in two steps.

Step 1: it is sufficient to prove
that, for every
h\in\mathbb{N}
, the function
F_{h}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})\to\mathbb{R}

(6.21)

F_{h}(\mu):=W_{2}(\hat{\pi}^{h}_{\sharp}\nu,\hat{\pi}^{h}_{\sharp}\mu)\quad\text{satisfies}\quad|{\mathrm{D}}F_{h}|_{\star,\mathscr{A}}\leq 1\quad\text{$\mathfrak{m}$-a.e.}

In fact, using the continuity property of the Wasserstein distance, it is
clear that for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})

(6.22)

\lim_{h\to\infty}F_{h}(\mu)=F(\mu),

so that it is enough to apply Theorem
2.3
(1)-(3) to obtain (
6.20
).

Step 2:
Let
h\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed and let us denote by
W_{2,h}
the Wasserstein distance on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{h})
; it is easy to check that

W_{2,h}(\pi^{h}_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\pi^{h}_{\sharp}\mu_{1})=W_{2}(\hat{\pi}^{h}_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\hat{\pi}^{h}_{\sharp}\mu_{1})\quad\text{ for every }\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H}).

Thus, if we define the function
\tilde{F}_{h}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{h})\to\mathbb{R}

as

\tilde{F}_{h}(\mu):=W_{2,h}(\pi^{h}_{\sharp}\nu,\mu)

we get that

F_{h}(\mu)=\tilde{F}_{h}(\pi^{h}_{\sharp}\mu).

We also introduce the measure
\mathfrak{m}_{h}
on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{h})
given by the push-forward of
\mathfrak{m}
through the (
1
-Lipschitz) map

P^{h}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{h})
defined as
P^{h}(\mu):=\pi^{h}_{\sharp}\mu
.
By Theorem
4.10
applied to
H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{h}),W_{2,h},\mathfrak{m}_{h})
,
we can find a sequence of cylinder functions
\tilde{F}_{h,n}\in\operatorname{FC}^{1}_{b}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{h})\right)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
such that

(6.23)

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{h,n}\to\tilde{F}_{h}\text{ in
$\mathfrak{m}_{h}$-measure},

(6.24)

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{h})}\tilde{F}_{h,n}\to g_{h}\quad\text{in
}L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{h}),\mathfrak{m}_{h})\quad\text{with }g_{h}\leq 1\text{ $\mathfrak{m}_{h}$-a.e.}

We thus consider the functions
F_{h,n}\in\operatorname{FC}^{1}_{b}\!\!\left(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})\right)

defined as in (
6.16
) by

(6.25)

F_{h,n}(\mu):=\tilde{F}_{h,n}(\pi^{h}_{\sharp}\mu)=\tilde{F}_{h,n}(P^{h}(\mu))\quad\text{for every }\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H}).

Since for every
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\Big(\big\{\mu:|F_{h,n}(\mu)-F_{h}(\mu)|>\varepsilon\big\}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{m}\Big(\big\{\mu:|\tilde{F}_{h,n}(P^{h}(\mu))-\tilde{F}_{h}(P^{h}(\mu))|>\varepsilon\big\}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{m}_{h}\Big(\big\{\mu:|\tilde{F}_{h,n}(\mu)-\tilde{F}_{h}(\mu)|>\varepsilon\big\}\Big),

(
6.23
) yields that
F_{h,n}\to F_{h}
in
\mathfrak{m}
-measure as

n\to\infty
.

On the other hand, (
6.17
) yields

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits F_{h,n}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{h})}\tilde{F}_{h,n}(P^{h}(\mu))

so that

\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits F_{h,n}\to g_{h}\circ P^{h}\quad\text{in }L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H}),\mathfrak{m})

and
g_{h}\circ P^{h}\leq 1

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})
.
By Theorem
2.3
(1)-(3), we obtain (
6.21
), concluding the proof.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

We observe that the results in Sections
4.2
and
5.1
can be extended to
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{M})
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{H})
in an analogous way.
